Unstable Vortices, Sharp Non-Uniqueness with Forcing, and Global Smooth Solutions for the SQG Equation
Authors: Ángel Castro, Daniel Faraco, Francisco Mengual, Marcos Solera
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We prove that non-uniqueness of weak solutions to the forced α α-SQG equation with Sobolev regularity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{s,p}$$\end{document}Ws,p in the supercritical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s < \alpha + \frac{2}{p}$$\end{document}s<α+2p, covering the 2D Euler equation (α = 0α=0), the Surface Quasi-Geostrophic equation (α = 1α=1), and the intermediate cases. A key step is the construction of smooth, compactly supported vortices that exhibit non-linear instability. As a by-product, we show that existence of global smooth solutions to the (unforced) α α-SQG equation that are neither rotating nor traveling.

Introduction and Main Results

We address the non-uniqueness of weak solutions to the generalized Surface Quasi-Geostrophic (α α-SQG) equation 1.1a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t\theta + v\cdot \nabla \theta =f, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tθ+v·∇θ=f,1.1b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v=-\nabla ^\perp \Lambda ^{\alpha -2}\theta ,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}v=-∇⊥Λα-2θ,1.1c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta |_{t=0} =\theta ^\circ , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ|t=0=θ∘, posed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2, for some given external force f and initial datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^\circ $$\end{document}θ∘, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda =(-\Delta )^{\nicefrac {1}{2}}$$\end{document}Λ=(-Δ)12. The velocity v(t, x) is recovered from the scalar θ (t,x)θ(t,x) through (1.1b), which we find convenient to refer to here as the α α-Biot-Savart law1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v(t,x) =C_\alpha \int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\frac{(x-y)^\perp }{|x-y|^{2+\alpha }}\theta (t,y)\,\textrm{d}y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v(t,x)=Cα∫R2(x-y)⊥|x-y|2+αθ(t,y)dy,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha =\frac{2^{\alpha }}{2\pi }\frac{\Gamma (1+\frac{\alpha }{2})}{\Gamma (1-\frac{\alpha }{2})}>0$$\end{document}Cα=2α2πΓ(1+α2)Γ(1-α2)>0.

The α α-SQG model was proposed by Córdoba, Fontelos, Mancho and Rodrigo [42] as an interpolation between the 2D Euler equation (α =0α=0) and the SQG equation (α =1α=1). The Euler equation describes the motion of an ideal, incompressible fluid, capturing vorticity evolution (see e.g. [107]). The SQG equation extends this concept to geophysical fluids, modeling temperature evolution in quasi-geostrophic atmospheric and oceanic flows (see, e.g. [116]). This family of active scalar equations has attracted significant interest since the seminal work of Constantin, Majda, and Tabak [40], which highlighted analogies between SQG and the 3D Euler equation. We will generally refer to θ θ as the temperature, unless specifically discussing 2D Euler, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta =\nabla ^\perp \cdot v$$\end{document}θ=∇⊥·v is the vorticity.

The potential lack of uniqueness is a central issue in fluid mechanics, relevant to both the question of determinism and its relationship with chaos and turbulence (see e.g. [89, 103, 106, 135]). From a mathematical point of view, given a parametrized family of function spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^s$$\end{document}Xs, where s typically quantifies the regularity of the solution θ θ, a natural question is to determine that1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} s_U\equiv \text { threshold for Uniqueness}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sU≡threshold for Uniqueness.Notice that (1.3) depends on α α and the baseline space. In terms of Sobolev regularity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in W^{s,p}$$\end{document}θ∈Ws,p, we have the following natural conjecture:1.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} s_U(\alpha ,p)=\alpha +\frac{2}{p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sU(α,p)=α+2p.The well-posedness of the α α-SQG equation has been proved in the subcritical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>\alpha +\frac{2}{p}$$\end{document}s>α+2p: globally for the 2D Euler equation by Bourguignon and Brezis [12], locally for the SQG equation by Chae [32], and locally, at least for p=2p=2, in the intermediate cases by Chae and Wu [35]. Heuristically, this is because θ θ is transported by a velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W^{s+1-\alpha ,p}\subset C^{1+}$$\end{document}v∈Ws+1-α,p⊂C1+. These results, based on standard energy methods, readily extend to the case with forcing in the natural space1.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f\in L^1([0,T],W^{s,p}\cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f∈L1([0,T],Ws,p∩H˙α-22),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}$$\end{document}H˙α-22 is the energy space associated with the Hamiltonian of the α α-SQG equation.

In the celebrated work [135], Yudovich proved that the 2D Euler equation (α =0α=0) is also globally well-posed at the critical point (s,p)=(0,∞ )(s,p)=(0,∞). In fact, Yudovich’s energy method can be adapted to prove uniqueness, assuming existence, along the critical line for α =0α=0, and other critical spaces such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{\alpha +1}$$\end{document}Hα+1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1. We caution that well-posedness implies uniqueness, but the converse is not true. Counterexamples by Elgindi and Jeong [64], Córdoba and Martínez-Zoroa [45], and Jeong and Kim [98] show critical exponents where ill-posedness occurs, with solutions losing regularity instantaneously in spaces where uniqueness holds. See also [44, 47, 48, 97] for ill-posedness in supercritical spaces, as well as [6, 17, 37] for the related issue of propagation of regularity.

Overall, the main obstruction for proving Conjecture (1.4) is to establish non-uniqueness in the supercritical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s<\alpha +\frac{2}{p}$$\end{document}s<α+2p. In this work, we show that this holds in the case with forcing (1.5).

Conjecture (1.4) remains an outstanding problem in the case f=0f=0. Remarkably, the construction of solutions that violate both uniqueness and Hamiltonian conservation has been successfully addressed in recent years through the method of convex integration, introduced into fluid dynamics by De Lellis and Székelyhidi [53]. See Sects. 1.5 and 1.6 for discussions on convex integration and conservation laws, as well as the reviews [22, 54–56]. Regarding the 2D Euler equation, θ θ is the vorticity, and the Hamiltonian equals the kinetic energy at the velocity level. In terms of Hölder regularity, Giri and Radu [80] recently solved the 2D Onsager conjecture by constructing non-trivial Euler flows \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\sim \Lambda ^{-1}\theta \in C^{\nicefrac {1}{3}-}$$\end{document}v∼Λ-1θ∈C13-. More recently, Bruè, Colombo, and Kumar [16] constructed the first non-trivial Euler flows with integrable vorticity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in L^{p}$$\end{document}θ∈Lp for some small p>1p>1 below the Onsager threshold. Regarding the SQG equation, the construction of non-trivial solutions was first established by Buckmaster, Shkoller, and Vicol [21] and was recently improved to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Lambda ^{-1} \theta \in C^{1-} $$\end{document}Λ-1θ∈C1- by Dai, Giri, and Radu [51], and by Isett and Looi [94], thereby solving the Onsager conjecture for SQG. More recently, Zhao [136] extended this result to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Lambda ^{-1} \theta \in C^{\frac{1+2\alpha }{3}-} $$\end{document}Λ-1θ∈C1+2α3- for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1. Notice that all the above convex integration constructions lie within the regime where the Hamiltonian does not need to be conserved. Since this is strictly contained within the broader regime where non-uniqueness is expected, it leaves a significant gap in the theory of non-uniqueness.

In the case with forcing, similar non-uniqueness results have been established using convex integration by Bulut, Huynh, and Palasek [25], and by Dai and Peng [52]. Indeed, for the 3D Euler equ. [24] it is possible to surpass the Onsager threshold up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C^{\nicefrac {1}{2}-}$$\end{document}v∈C12-. However, to the best of our knowledge, non-unique solutions with some Sobolev or Hölder regularity at the level of θ θ had not been constructed until now for the α α-SQG equation, even in the case with forcing.

This work is devoted to exploring another mechanism for non-uniqueness, based on the self-similar instability approach introduced by Jia and Šverák in the context of the 3D Navier-Stokes equ. [99]. In a remarkable pair of papers [129, 130], Vishik successfully established non-uniqueness for the forced 2D Euler equation (α =0α=0) below the Yudovich well-posedness class (s,p)=(0,∞ )(s,p)=(0,∞). The present paper extends Vishik’s non-uniqueness theorem to the forced α α-SQG equations for the full supercritical Sobolev regime and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1.

Theorem 1.1

(Sobolev non-uniqueness) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1, s≥ 0s≥0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le p\le \infty $$\end{document}1≤p≤∞ satisfy1.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} s<\alpha +\frac{2}{p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}s<α+2p.There exist T>0T>0 and a force (1.5) such that there are uncountably many solutions1.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _\epsilon \in L^\infty ([0,T],W^{s,p}\cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θϵ∈L∞([0,T],Ws,p∩H˙α-22)to the α α-SQG equation (1.1) starting from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^\circ =0$$\end{document}θ∘=0.

As we will explain in more detail in Sect. 3, Vishik’s result (Theorem 3.2) corresponds to the specific case α = s = 0α=s=0. Indeed, it is a particular case of a refined version of Theorem 1.1, namely, our Theorem 3.1.

Improvements and Corollaries

The regularity of the force and the solutions can be described in various other function spaces. Thus, Theorem 1.1 allows for certain improvements and by-products, which we summarize below and will later state as theorems and corollaries in the introduction.

Firstly, let us remark that both the force f and the main solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _0$$\end{document}θ0 from Theorem 1.1 belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_c^\infty $$\end{document}Cc∞ for positive times. Moreover, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N, the other solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _\epsilon $$\end{document}θϵ can be upgraded to be in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_c^{k}$$\end{document}Cck for positive times. These estimates inevitably deteriorate as t→ 0t→0. Although spatial regularity cannot exceed the critical line, temporal regularity can easily be improved to Lipschitz, or even enhanced with additional derivatives, by choosing the parameter a appropriately in Sect. 2.1.

As a consequence of the smoothness of our solutions, Theorem 1.1 can be generalized to any space that is scaling-supercritical with respect to the α α-SQG equation and, at the same time, includes smooth, compactly supported functions, such as Besov or Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. In this work, we state the theorems in the context of classical Sobolev spaces for the sake of simplicity.

In the scale of Lebesgue spaces (s=0s=0), Theorem 1.1 shows non-uniqueness within the DiPerna-Majda existence class for the 2D Euler equation [60], which corresponds to Vishik’s non-uniqueness theorem, as well as within the Resnick-Marchand existence class for the SQG equation [108, 118], and the Chae-Constantin-Córdoba-Gancedo existence class for the generalized SQG equation [33], all in the presence of forcing.

In the scale of Hölder spaces, which we express as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{-1}\theta \in C^\gamma $$\end{document}Λ-1θ∈Cγ to facilitate comparison with the Onsager exponent, we have the following natural conjecture1.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \gamma _U(\alpha )=1+\alpha . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}γU(α)=1+α.Similarly to the Sobolev case, in the subcritical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma > 1 + \alpha $$\end{document}γ>1+α, we would have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \sim \Lambda ^{\alpha - 1} \theta \in C^{1+}$$\end{document}v∼Λα-1θ∈C1+. Well-posedness in subcritical Hölder spaces has been established for the 2D Euler equation by Wolibner [133] and Hölder [88], and for the SQG equation by Wu [134] and by Ambrose, Cozzi, Erickson, and Kelliher [3]. Uniqueness can be deduced, assuming existence, in some critical Hölder spaces as a consequence of the weak-strong uniqueness principle (see e.g. [132]). In contrast, there are known counterexamples of such critical Hölder spaces that exhibit strong ill-posedness. This was first established by Bourgain and Li for the Euler equation [11], and also by Elgindi and Masmoudi [65]. These results were later extended to both the SQG and generalized SQG equations by Córdoba and Martínez-Zoroa [45, 46].

By the extension of Morrey’s inequality to fractional Sobolev spaces [58], we deduce, as a corollary of Theorem 1.1, non-uniqueness for the full supercritical Hölder regime in the case with forcing.

Corollary 1.1

(Hölder non-uniqueness) In the setting of Theorem 1.1, for every fixed\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \gamma <1+\alpha , $$\end{document}γ<1+α,the solutions and the force can be chosen to satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Lambda ^{-1}\theta _\epsilon \in L^\infty ([0,T],C^\gamma ), \quad \quad \Lambda ^{-1}f\in L^1([0,T],C^\gamma ). $$\end{document}Λ-1θϵ∈L∞([0,T],Cγ),Λ-1f∈L1([0,T],Cγ).

Finally, we emphasize that our solutions satisfy both the Hamiltonian identity (Corollary 1.4) and the renormalization property (Corollary 1.5) in the presence of forcing. See Sects. 1.5 and 1.6 for the precise definitions of the Hamiltonian (1.11) and the renormalization (1.19), respectively.

Up to this point, we have discussed the improvements and corollaries regarding non-uniqueness with forcing. A key intermediate step of independent mathematical and physical interest involves the explicit construction of smooth, compactly supported vortices that exhibit non-linear instability (Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.2). As a corollary, we show the existence of global smooth solutions to the (unforced) α α-SQG equation that are n-fold symmetric, non-stationary, and converge to a radial temperature as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}t→∞ (Corollary 1.3). See Sect. 2 for the precise definition of vortices and n-fold symmetry.

Main Novelties

The starting point of our work is the groundbreaking result of Vishik on the non-uniqueness of solutions to the forced 2D Euler equ. See [129, 130] for the original papers and the book [2] by Albritton, Bruè, Colombo, De Lellis, Giri, Janisch, and Kwon for a careful and detailed exposition. Vishik’s work raised the question of the validity of analogous results for the more singular generalized SQG equations, and more importantly, for the SQG equation itself. However, it appears quite challenging to implement Vishik’s argument for these non-linear and singular counterparts of the 2D Euler equation. Motivated by this, we devised an alternative approach for the 2D Euler equ. in [31], which not only substantially simplifies Vishik’s proof but also appears robust enough to be adapted to other situations. This is what we have accomplished in the current paper. However, essentially every step of the construction requires new ideas, with the SQG case being particularly delicate due to the non-compact relationship between the velocity and the advected scalar. We will next explain the parallels and innovations we consider noteworthy.

The proof of Theorem 1.1 follows the general strategy presented in [31], which is that of [2, 129, 130] except for the construction of the unstable vortex. This approach consists of four steps: Construct a piecewise constant unstable vortex.Regularize the unstable vortex mantaining instability.Prove that the regularized vortex is self-similarly unstable.Prove that the regularized vortex is non-linearly unstable.Let us summarize the main differences with respect to our proof in the 2D Euler case: While for α =0α=0 Step (1) reduces to an elementary computation, for α >0α>0 the non-locality introduced by the α α-Biot-Savart law necessitates dealing with a parametric integral that cannot be computed. Despite this lack of explicitness, we are able to show that there are piecewise constant unstable vortices by obtaining precise asymptotics in Sect. 4.1.Step (2) requires correctly expanding the linear stability equation into a leading term and a lower-order term that behaves like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\varepsilon ^{1-\alpha }}{1-\alpha }$$\end{document}ε1-α1-α. In the limiting case α =1α=1, the roles of these operators are reversed, completely changing the nature of the problem. In Sect. 5.4, we show that even in this situation it is possible to handle the operators and apply a fixed point argument to find a regularized unstable vortex.Step (3) involves decomposing the linearization into an operator that generates a contraction semigroup and a compact operator K. However, this operator K loses compactness for SQG. We bypass this obstacle in Sect. 6.6 by decomposing K into a skew-adjoint operator, which preserves the growth bound of the semigroup, along with a compact commutator.Step (4) is carried out in [2, 31, 129, 130] through energy estimates on first-order derivatives in polar coordinates, applied in the correct order. Here, achieving the full supercritical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s < \alpha + \frac{2}{p}$$\end{document}s<α+2p requires working with higher-order derivatives. The resulting energy space consists of functions that vanish at the origin. Moreover, to manage the many terms, we introduce an inductive argument in Sect. 7.5, which significantly streamlines the proof. In addition, a delicate estimate involving the commutator is necessary in the SQG case.During the finalization of this work, a clever trick discovered by Golovkin in the 1960s [82] was highlighted by Dolce and Mescolini [61], showing that by absorbing the quadratic interaction of the linear instability into the forcing term, it is possible to bypass the non-linear instability step and construct two distinct solutions (instead of uncountably many). In Sect. 3.3, we revisit this trick in the context of the α α-SQG equation.

Construct a piecewise constant unstable vortex.

Construct a piecewise constant unstable vortex.

Regularize the unstable vortex mantaining instability.

Regularize the unstable vortex mantaining instability.

Prove that the regularized vortex is self-similarly unstable.

Prove that the regularized vortex is self-similarly unstable.

Prove that the regularized vortex is non-linearly unstable.

Prove that the regularized vortex is non-linearly unstable.

While for α =0α=0 Step (1) reduces to an elementary computation, for α >0α>0 the non-locality introduced by the α α-Biot-Savart law necessitates dealing with a parametric integral that cannot be computed. Despite this lack of explicitness, we are able to show that there are piecewise constant unstable vortices by obtaining precise asymptotics in Sect. 4.1.

While for α =0α=0 Step (1) reduces to an elementary computation, for α >0α>0 the non-locality introduced by the α α-Biot-Savart law necessitates dealing with a parametric integral that cannot be computed. Despite this lack of explicitness, we are able to show that there are piecewise constant unstable vortices by obtaining precise asymptotics in Sect. 4.1.

Step (2) requires correctly expanding the linear stability equation into a leading term and a lower-order term that behaves like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\varepsilon ^{1-\alpha }}{1-\alpha }$$\end{document}ε1-α1-α. In the limiting case α =1α=1, the roles of these operators are reversed, completely changing the nature of the problem. In Sect. 5.4, we show that even in this situation it is possible to handle the operators and apply a fixed point argument to find a regularized unstable vortex.

Step (2) requires correctly expanding the linear stability equation into a leading term and a lower-order term that behaves like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\varepsilon ^{1-\alpha }}{1-\alpha }$$\end{document}ε1-α1-α. In the limiting case α =1α=1, the roles of these operators are reversed, completely changing the nature of the problem. In Sect. 5.4, we show that even in this situation it is possible to handle the operators and apply a fixed point argument to find a regularized unstable vortex.

Step (3) involves decomposing the linearization into an operator that generates a contraction semigroup and a compact operator K. However, this operator K loses compactness for SQG. We bypass this obstacle in Sect. 6.6 by decomposing K into a skew-adjoint operator, which preserves the growth bound of the semigroup, along with a compact commutator.

Step (3) involves decomposing the linearization into an operator that generates a contraction semigroup and a compact operator K. However, this operator K loses compactness for SQG. We bypass this obstacle in Sect. 6.6 by decomposing K into a skew-adjoint operator, which preserves the growth bound of the semigroup, along with a compact commutator.

Step (4) is carried out in [2, 31, 129, 130] through energy estimates on first-order derivatives in polar coordinates, applied in the correct order. Here, achieving the full supercritical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s < \alpha + \frac{2}{p}$$\end{document}s<α+2p requires working with higher-order derivatives. The resulting energy space consists of functions that vanish at the origin. Moreover, to manage the many terms, we introduce an inductive argument in Sect. 7.5, which significantly streamlines the proof. In addition, a delicate estimate involving the commutator is necessary in the SQG case.

Step (4) is carried out in [2, 31, 129, 130] through energy estimates on first-order derivatives in polar coordinates, applied in the correct order. Here, achieving the full supercritical regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s < \alpha + \frac{2}{p}$$\end{document}s<α+2p requires working with higher-order derivatives. The resulting energy space consists of functions that vanish at the origin. Moreover, to manage the many terms, we introduce an inductive argument in Sect. 7.5, which significantly streamlines the proof. In addition, a delicate estimate involving the commutator is necessary in the SQG case.

Nonetheless, our non-linear instability approach has several remarkable by-products. First, it rigorously proves the existence of non-linearly unstable vortices for the unforced α α-SQG equation. Second, it provides a robust framework for transitioning from linear to non-linear instability in arbitrarily high Sobolev regularity. Third, by reversing the arrow of time, it shows the existence of global smooth solutions that are neither rotating nor traveling. See Sects. 1.3 and 1.4 for more details. Additionally, it could be useful for showing the potential lack of uniqueness in the absence of forcing, as it offers a more precise representation of the α α-SQG dynamics.

Unstable Vortices

The stability analysis of simple steady states in the 2D Euler equ. is a classical problem that dates back to the works of Kelvin [126], Rayleigh [117], Orr [114], Taylor [125], and many others. Although some explicit shear flows were known to be unstable (see e.g. [62]), to the best of our knowledge, the first rigorous construction of an unstable vortex was achieved by Vishik (see also [2]). Vishik’s approach is based on bifurcation from neutral modes, a method that traces back to Tollmien’s work on shear flows [127]. This technique was later rigorously applied by Lin to shear flows in a bounded channel and in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2, and to rotating flows in an annulus [104].

Although there was numerical evidence supporting the existence of unstable vortices for the α α-SQG equation (see e.g. [87]), to the best of our knowledge, they had not yet been rigorously constructed for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0<α≤1. We refer to the work of Friedlander and Shvydkoy [76] for the analysis of the spectrum of SQG and the existence of unstable eigenvalues for an oscillating shear flow.

In this work, we adapt our construction of smooth, compactly supported, unstable vortices for the 2D Euler equ. [31] to the full range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1. In particular, this rigorously proves the existence of non-linearly unstable vortices for the α α-SQG equation with f=0f=0. Moreover, we prove a stronger property, namely that the vortex exhibits non-uniqueness at t=-∞ t=-∞ in the sense of the following statement:

Theorem 1.2

(Non-uniqueness at t=-∞ t=-∞) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1 and n≥ 2n≥2. There exists a vortex\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \bar{\Theta }\in C_c^\infty \cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}, $$\end{document}Θ¯∈Cc∞∩H˙α-22,such that, for every m>α +1m>α+1, there is an n-fold symmetric solution to the (unforced) α α-SQG equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \theta \in C((-\infty ,0],H^m\cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}), $$\end{document}θ∈C((-∞,0],Hm∩H˙α-22),satisfying\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \theta (t)-\bar{\Theta }\Vert _{H^m}\sim e^{\Re \lambda t}, $$\end{document}‖θ(t)-Θ¯‖Hm∼eℜλt,as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}t→-∞, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}λ∈C with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Re \lambda >0$$\end{document}ℜλ>0. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \theta (t)\Vert _{\dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}} =\Vert \bar{\Theta }\Vert _{\dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}} $$\end{document}‖θ(t)‖H˙α-22=‖Θ¯‖H˙α-22 for all t≤ 0t≤0.

Above, the complex number λ λ corresponds to the eigenvalue of the linear instability associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯. By starting at a finite time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0<0$$\end{document}t0<0 sufficiently far back in time, the non-uniqueness at t=-∞ t=-∞ (Theorem 1.2) implies the non-linear instability as it is usually stated (Corollary 1.2). Notice that, by shifting the time interval, we can take that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0=t_0<T$$\end{document}0=t0<T.

Corollary 1.2

(Non-linearly unstable vortex) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1 and n≥ 2n≥2. There exists δ > 0δ>0 such that, for every ɛ >0ε>0 and m>α +1m>α+1, there exist T>0T>0 and an n-fold symmetric solution to the (unforced) α α-SQG equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \theta \in C([0,T],H^m\cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}), $$\end{document}θ∈C([0,T],Hm∩H˙α-22),satisfying1.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \theta (0)-\bar{\Theta }\Vert _{H^m} \le \varepsilon , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖θ(0)-Θ¯‖Hm≤ε,and1.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \theta (T)-\bar{\Theta }\Vert _{H^m}\ge \delta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖θ(T)-Θ¯‖Hm≥δ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is the vortex from Theorem 1.2.

Corollary 1.2 shows energy pump of certain solutions to the α α-SQG equation, aligning with the work of Kiselev and Nazarov [101] for SQG. Double exponential growth of the gradient of the vorticity was established by Kiselev and Šverák for the 2D Euler equation [102] in the unit disc. Exponential growth of the gradient of the vorticity was provided for 2D Euler in the torus by Zlatoš [137]. For SQG, He and Kiselev [86] showed exponential growth of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{1,\gamma }$$\end{document}C1,γ-norm of the temperature in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2.

As we introduced in Sect. 1.2, the construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is divided into several steps. The first step involves constructing a piecewise constant unstable vortex. While this idea was inspired by previous constructions for shear flows in the 2D Euler equation (see e.g. [62, Chapter 4]), we remark that our construction operates at the level of the temperature, in contrast to the classical Rayleigh stability equation, which deals with the stream function. Secondly, we find a suitable regularized unstable vortex through a fixed-point argument. The idea that regularized unstable shear flows retain their instability was proposed by Grenier for the Euler equation [84]. In this work, we rigorously prove that this is the case for vortices in the α α-SQG equation. Finally, the last step consists of proving that the non-linear effects are negligible within a certain time interval.

In [84] Grenier developed a program to transition from linear to non-linear instability. In particular, Grenier showed that, given a linearly unstable smooth shear flow for the Euler equation, it is possible to deduce the properties (1.9) and (1.10). The transition from linear to non-linear instability has been also analyzed by Bulut and Dong [23] for the SQG equation with dissipation. Grenier’s strategy for the Euler equation appears robust enough to be applied to other equations, such as the α α-SQG equation. Thus, by combining this approach with our Theorem 3.3, it is plausible that this could provide an alternative proof of Corollary 1.2. In order to upgrade the non-linear instability to non-uniqueness at t=-∞ t=-∞ (Theorem 1.2), we apply a delicate bootstrapping argument to obtain additional exponential decay uniformly in time.

Recently, Dolce and Mescolini [61] presented another flexible strategy for constructing linearly unstable vortices for the 2D Euler equation. An interesting question is whether a similar variety of unstable vortices also exists for α >0α>0. It is worth noting that the non-uniqueness of Leray-Hopf solutions to the forced 3D Navier-Stokes equation was established by Albritton, Bruè, and Colombo [1] by carefully adapting Vishik’s unstable vortex in the axisymmetric setting. The unstable vortices constructed in [31, 61], while different from Vishik’s, satisfy the conditions of [1], offering an alternative example of non-unique Leray-Hopf solutions with forcing.

Global Smooth Solutions

A significant distinction between the 2D Euler equation (α =0α=0) and the SQG equation (α =1α=1) is the potential lack of global well-posedness in the SQG case, as well as in the intermediate cases (0<α <10<α<1). Whether the SQG equation develops finite-time singularities remains an outstanding open problem in mathematical fluid mechanics (see e.g. [34, 40, 50]).

In this regard, the construction of global smooth solutions that exhibit non-trivial dynamics has attracted considerable attention from the mathematical community in recent years. In [27], the first author, in collaboration with Córdoba and Gómez-Serrano, constructed the first non-trivial family of global smooth solutions to the SQG equation, consisting of rotating 3-fold symmetric solutions with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^4$$\end{document}C4 regularity bifurcating from a smooth annular profile. The argument required a computer-assisted proof, which could potentially be adapted to produce more regular n-fold symmetric solutions. Smooth rotating solutions bifurcating from vortex points can be found in [4]. The construction of smooth traveling solutions has been carried out by Gravejat and Smets [83], by Godard-Cadillac [81], as well as by Ao, Dávila, del Pino, Musso, and Wei [4].

In [43], Córdoba, Gómez-Serrano, and Ionescu constructed global solutions to the α α-SQG patch equation consisting of non-stationary solutions which converge to the stationary half-plane patch as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}t→∞. In this line of thought, another approach to constructing families of global smooth solutions around steady states could involve the mechanism of inviscid damping. This has been successfully applied to the 2D Euler equation for perturbations of the Couette flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }=-1$$\end{document}Θ¯=-1 by Bedrossian and Masmoudi [8], and by Ionescu and Jia [90], and later extended to general monotonic shear flows by Ionescu and Jia [91] and by Masmoudi and Zhao [109]. Unfortunately, recent work by Gómez-Serrano, Ionescu and Jia (see [92, Section 3]) suggests that the approach of inviscid damping, with the aim of producing globally smooth solutions, fails for the Couette flow for any α > 0α>0, due to a forward cascade that leads to a loss of regularity in finite time. Therefore, for α > 0α>0, it remains unclear whether a smooth steady state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ to the α α-SQG equation can exist with a (small) open neighborhood of perturbations that converge to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}t→∞, or even whether these perturbations produce global smooth solutions.

In this work, we show that by reversing the arrow of time in Theorem 1.2, there exist particular perturbations of the vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ that converge to it as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}t→∞. As a result, we establish the existence of global smooth solutions to the α α-SQG equation that are neither rotating nor traveling.

Corollary 1.3

(Global smooth solutions) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1, n≥ 2n≥2 and m>α +1m>α+1. There exists a global solution to the (unforced) α α-SQG equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \theta \in C([0,\infty ),H^m\cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}), $$\end{document}θ∈C([0,∞),Hm∩H˙α-22),which is n-fold symmetric, non-stationary, and converges to the vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }\in C_c^\infty \cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}$$\end{document}Θ¯∈Cc∞∩H˙α-22 from Theorem 1.2 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^m$$\end{document}Hm as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}t→∞.

Notice that our solutions are defined for positive times, thus strictly speaking they are global forward in time. We remark that our approach allows for the construction of global solutions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^m$$\end{document}Hm for any fixed m>α +1m>α+1. However, it is easy to see within the construction that a Beale-Kato-Majda argument implies that the solutions remain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\infty $$\end{document}C∞ for all t≥ 0t≥0.

In these results we have used m (instead of s) to distinguish it from the supercritical case (s<α +1s<α+1) and to facilitate comparison with Sect. 7, where everything is written in terms of the higher-order norms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^m$$\end{document}Hm. Although our non-linear analysis allows us to control any Sobolev norm, we chose to state the results in the Hilbert case for simplicity and because it is more commonly used in the α α-SQG setting.

Recently, the existence of self-similar spirals was established by García and Gómez-Serrano [78] for the α α-SQG equation, with 0<α <10<α<1, and by Shao, Wei, and Zhang for the 2D Euler equation [120], building on the previous work of Elling [67]. In these examples it holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in L^p$$\end{document}θ∈Lp, leading to a promising scenario for non-uniqueness without forcing. Other promising scenarios based on symmetry breaking and self-similarity for the 2D Euler equation have been proposed recently by Bressan, Shen, and Murray [13, 14], as well as by Elgindi, Murray, and Said [66].

Hamiltonian Identity

The α α-SQG equation formally possesses a large class of conservation laws. The Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}=\mathcal {H}_\alpha $$\end{document}H=Hα,1.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {H}(t) =\frac{1}{2}\Vert \theta (t)\Vert _{\dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}}^2 =\frac{1}{2}\Vert v(t)\Vert _{\dot{H}^{-\frac{\alpha }{2}}}^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(t)=12‖θ(t)‖H˙α-222=12‖v(t)‖H˙-α22,is particularly meaningful because the α α-SQG equation can be interpreted as geodesics with respect to the metric (1.11) on the Lie group of volume-preserving diffeomorphisms (see e.g. [5, 7, 21, 111, 131]). By a short energy estimate, it is straightforward to check that smooth solutions to the α α-SQG equation satisfy the Hamiltonian identity1.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}\mathcal {H} =\langle f,\theta \rangle _{\dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtH=⟨f,θ⟩H˙α-22.In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H is conserved for classical solutions in the case f=0f=0. Similarly to the question of uniqueness, the lack of Hamiltonian conservation is a cornerstone in the theory of turbulence (see e.g. [77]). Analogously to (1.3), a natural question is to determine1.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} s_H\equiv \text { threshold for Hamiltonian identity}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sH≡threshold for Hamiltonian identity,which again depends on α α and the baseline space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^s$$\end{document}Xs.

In terms of Hölder regularity, which is usually expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda ^{-1}\theta \in C^\gamma $$\end{document}Λ-1θ∈Cγ in the α α-SQG setting, Onsager conjectured in the celebrated work [113] that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _H = \nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}γH=13 for the Euler equation, which is dimensionless at the velocity level. The conservative part of Onsager’s conjecture was proved by Constantin, E, and Titi [38], building on a partial result by Eyink [69]. Onsager’s conjecture can be extended to the α α-SQG equation as follows1.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \gamma _H(\alpha )=\frac{1+2\alpha }{3}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}γH(α)=1+2α3.As we mentioned earlier in the introduction, the non-conservative part of the Onsager-type conjecture (1.14) has been recently proven using convex integration for the 2D Euler equation [80], the SQG equation [51, 94], and the intermediate cases [136]. The Onsager conjecture for the 3D Euler equation was previously solved by Isett [93], by Buckmaster, De Lellis, Székelyhidi, and Vicol [19] for dissipative solutions, and by Giri, Kwon, and Novack [79] for locally dissipative solutions in Besov spaces. Additionally, see the work of Masmoudi, Novack, and Vicol [20, 112] on the Hilbert case. We also refer to the works for Scheffer [119] and Shnirelman [121] for the first constructions of non-unique Euler flows. Notice that all these results prove non-uniqueness up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _H=\frac{1+2\alpha }{3}$$\end{document}γH=1+2α3, leaving a gap in the conjectured \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _U=1+\alpha $$\end{document}γU=1+α in the case without forcing.

In the context of Lebesgue spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in L^p$$\end{document}θ∈Lp, Duchon and Robert were the first to prove that 2D Euler vorticities with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p > \nicefrac {3}{2}$$\end{document}p>32 conserve the Hamiltonian [63]. This was extended to the borderline case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p = \nicefrac {3}{2}$$\end{document}p=32 by Cheskidov, Lopes Filho, Nussenzveig Lopes, and Shvydkoy [36]. These results lead to the natural conjecture for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_H(\alpha )$$\end{document}pH(α) in the case of the 2D Euler equation (α =0α=0)1.15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_H(0)=\nicefrac {3}{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pH(0)=32.The first non-conservative results in weak Lebesgue spaces were obtained by Bruè and Colombo in Lorentz spaces [15], and by Modena and Buck in Hardy spaces [18]. Remarkably, Bruè, Colombo, and Kumar [16] recently constructed non-trivial 2D Euler vorticities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in L^{1 + \varepsilon }$$\end{document}θ∈L1+ε. As already pointed out by the authors, this yields the first example of 2D Euler flows with uniformly integrable vorticity that are not vanishing viscosity solutions. A natural question is whether this new convex integration scheme can be extended to reach (1.15).

As we discussed for the Hölder case, even if the Onsager exponent (1.15) can be achieved with these techniques, there would still be a gap to the conjectured Yudovich exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_U(0) = \infty $$\end{document}pU(0)=∞ in the case without forcing. In this regard, the third author constructed initial data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^\circ \in L^p$$\end{document}θ∘∈Lp, for any fixed p < ∞ p<∞, admitting infinitely many solutions with non-increasing Hamiltonian [110], thus showcasing the sharpness of the weak-strong uniqueness principle and Yudovich’s proof of uniqueness. Unfortunately, the vorticity information is lost for positive times in [110], as the weak solutions are only understood at the velocity level.

Concerning the SQG equation (α =1α=1) Isett and Vicol [96] proved Hamiltonian conservation provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in L^3$$\end{document}θ∈L3. On the one hand, this implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_H(1)\le 3$$\end{document}pH(1)≤3. On the other hand, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^0\subset L^3$$\end{document}C0⊂L3 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2, this solves the conservative part of the Onsager-type conjecture (1.14) for α =1α=1. See also the recent work of Isett and Ma [95] on conservation laws for α α-SQG.

By the Sobolev embedding theorem, a natural extension of Conjecture (1.15) for 2D Euler vorticities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in W^{s,p}$$\end{document}θ∈Ws,p would be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_H(0, p) = \frac{2}{p} - \frac{4}{3}$$\end{document}sH(0,p)=2p-43. A similar exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_H(\alpha ,p)$$\end{document}sH(α,p) could be derived for the general case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1. The problem of constructing non-unique solutions with some Sobolev regularity remains widely open for the α α-SQG equation without forcing.

The Hamiltonian structure of α α-SQG makes it a rare and significant example of active scalars. Notably, the method of convex integration has produced much more regular solutions when applied to other active scalars that lack a Hamiltonian structure. A key prototype is the IPM equation, known for its gradient flow structure [115] and its role as a model to address fluid instabilities through convex integration. In this regard see e.g. [26, 28–30, 41, 75, 124]. In fact, very general classes of active scalars, explicitly excluding SQG, can be treated using variants of convex integration, yielding bounded [122] and even continuous [96, 136] turbulent solutions.

Finally, we remark that vanishing viscosity solutions to the 2D Navier-Stokes equations with uniform integrability are known to conserve the Hamiltonian. This was proved in [36] for p > 1p>1, and recently extended to the borderline case p = 1p=1 by De Rosa and Park [57]. A similar result was obtained by Constantin and Ignatova in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L^2$$\end{document}L2 for the dissipative SQG equation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2 [39], and previously in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 by Berselli [10]. This discrepancy in Hamiltonian conservation between ideal solutions and ideal limits has also been observed in other fluid models, such as 3D Magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) by the second author and Lindberg [71].

Therefore, the search for physically realizable solutions with uniformly integrable θ θ for the 2D Euler and SQG equations involves imposing that the Hamiltonian is conserved, or more generally the Hamiltonian identity in the presence of forcing. As a consequence of the regularity of the solutions in Theorem 3.1, we deduce that our solutions satisfy this property. Thus, satisfying the Hamiltonian identity is not a criterion for uniqueness, at least in the case with forcing.

Corollary 1.4

In the setting of Theorem 1.1, the solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _\epsilon $$\end{document}θϵ and the force f satisfy the Hamiltonian identity (1.12).

Renormalization Property

The Hamiltonian is not the only conserved quantity in α α-SQG. Since this is a transport equation, any functional of the temperature is formally preserved by the flow. Given a classical solution θ θ and a function β β, the temperature Casimir1.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_\beta (t):=\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\beta (\theta (t,x))\,\textrm{d}x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cβ(t):=∫R2β(θ(t,x))dx,satisfies the identity1.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\,\textrm{d}}{\,\textrm{d}t} C_\beta =\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\beta '(\theta )f\,\textrm{d}x. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtCβ=∫R2β′(θ)fdx.In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\beta $$\end{document}Cβ is conserved in the case f=0f=0. The Casimir temperature associated with the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =\frac{1}{q}|\cdot |^q$$\end{document}β=1q|·|q for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le q<\infty $$\end{document}1≤q<∞ corresponds to the q-energy (or momentum)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {E}_q(t) =\frac{1}{q}\Vert \theta (t)\Vert _{L^q}^q. $$\end{document}Eq(t)=1q‖θ(t)‖Lqq.In this regard, a solution to the α α-SQG equation θ θ satisfies the q-energy balance if1.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\,\textrm{d}}{\,\textrm{d}t} \mathcal {E}_q =\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\theta |\theta |^{q-2}f\,\textrm{d}x. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEq=∫R2θ|θ|q-2fdx.For the 2D Euler equation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}=\mathcal {E}_2$$\end{document}E=E2 represents the enstrophy, which plays a crucial role in 2D turbulence (see e.g. [37, 70, 105]). Indeed, in turbulent regimes, it is plausible for the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H to be conserved, energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_q$$\end{document}Eq to dissipate, and non-uniqueness to arise. The existence of two quadratic invariants, both preserved by smooth solutions−one more robust than the other−forms the precise framework of the celebrated self-organization conjecture [85]. Vorticity evolution in 2D Euler is a prime example of self-organization, and the α α-SQG equation aligns with this theory, at least at a formal level.

Recent studies have implemented convex integration schemes compatible with conservation of magnetic helicity in MHD [9, 72–74], which represents another canonical example of self-organization. Following this perspective, it is plausible that convex integration could be effective in regimes where the Hamiltonian is conserved for the α α-SQG equation but dissipation of q-energies is feasible. Thus, whether convex integration can be applied in the Hamiltonian conservation regime, or whether it can be shown to be impossible, remains a compelling and challenging open problem.

The Casimirs balance can be described locally through the concept of renormalized solutions. A solution to the α α-SQG equation θ θ satisfies the renormalization property if1.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t\beta (\theta ) +v\cdot \nabla (\beta (\theta )) =\beta '(\theta )f \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tβ(θ)+v·∇(β(θ))=β′(θ)fholds in the sense of distributions for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in C_c^1(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}β∈Cc1(R); that is,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _0^T\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}(\beta (\theta )(\partial _t\varphi +v\cdot \nabla \varphi )+\beta '(\theta )f\varphi )\,\textrm{d}x\,\textrm{d}t =-\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\beta (\theta ^\circ (x))\varphi (0,x)\,\textrm{d}x $$\end{document}∫0T∫R2(β(θ)(∂tφ+v·∇φ)+β′(θ)fφ)dxdt=-∫R2β(θ∘(x))φ(0,x)dxfor all test function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C_c^1([0,T)\times \mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}φ∈Cc1([0,T)×R2). Notice that the concept of weak (or distributional) solution corresponds to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow \textrm{id}$$\end{document}β→id. By integrating (1.19) and applying that v is weakly divergence-free, we recover the Casimir identity (1.17). Formally, ignoring the singularity at zero and its growth at infinity, we can also recover the q-energy balance (1.18).

Analogously to (1.13), a natural question is to determine the threshold for the renormalization property, as well as the q-energy balance. For the 2D Euler equation, Crippa and Spirito shown that vanishing viscosity limits are renormalized [49] provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in L^p$$\end{document}θ∈Lp with p>1p>1. In fact, by the DiPerna-Lions theory [59] any weak solution with vorticity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 is renormalized. However, it might be that large moments are dissipated in the vanishing viscosity limit [37].

As a consequence of the regularity of the solutions of Theorem 3.1, they can be shown to be renormalized, and thus being renormalized is not a criteria for uniqueness, at least in the case with forcing. Moreover, they satisfy the q-energy balance, provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_q$$\end{document}Eq is well-defined.

Corollary 1.5

In the setting of Theorem 1.1, the solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _\epsilon $$\end{document}θϵ and the force f satisfy the renormalization property, and also the q-energy balance for q moments as long as they are well defined.

As we already mentioned, both Corollaries 1.4 and 1.5 follow from the regularity obtained in Theorem 3.1, by taking ɛ > 0ε>0 small enough if necessary, along with the conservative results discussed in Sects. 1.5 and 1.6. Alternatively, they can be deduced more directly as follows. On the one hand, the force and the solutions are classical for positive times, so they trivially satisfy all the conservation laws for t>0t>0. On the other hand, the solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _\epsilon $$\end{document}θϵ converge to zero as t → 0t→0 in the corresponding Sobolev spaces (Proposition 2.2), which is sufficient to verify that the conservation laws are also satisfied at t=0t=0.
