Finally, we remark that most of the literature mentioned in this section refers to the periodic box \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2, while our theorems are stated in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2. Although our solutions can be shown to have compact support, the corresponding velocity does not necessarily. A natural problem is to extend our construction to the periodic box, as well as to bounded domains.

Organization of the Paper

We start Sect. 2 by deriving the stability equation in self-similar coordinates. Next, we outline the proof of the main theorems in Sect. 3. In Sect. 4 we construct the piecewise constant unstable vortex, and we find a regularization in Sect. 5. Finally, in Sects. 6 and 7 we prove that the vortex is also self-similarly and non-linearly unstable, respectively.

Stability Equation in Self-Similar Coordinates

In this section, we provide some preliminary definitions and results that will be used throughout the paper. Firstly, we rewrite the α α-SQG equation in self-similar coordinates. Secondly, we derive both the linear and non-linear stability equations around vortices.

Self-Similar Coordinates

Remark 2.1

Following the notation in [1, 31], given an object related to the α α-SQG equation, the corresponding object in self-similar coordinates will be denoted by the same letter in uppercase. The only exception is the vortex (3.4). Nonetheless, notice that the case b=0b=0 in (2.3) agrees with α α-SQG in the original coordinates, while b>0b>0 represents the equation in self-similar coordinates.

In the next proposition we show how the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{s,p}$$\end{document}Ws,p norm behaves under the self-similar change of variables (2.1). We recall that, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}s∈R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le p\le \infty $$\end{document}1≤p≤∞, the fractional Sobolev space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{s,p}=W^{s,p}(\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}Ws,p=Ws,p(R2) is defined by the normwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{f}$$\end{document}f^ denotes the Fourier transform\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \hat{f}(k)=\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}f(x)e^{-ix\cdot k}\,\textrm{d}x, $$\end{document}f^(k)=∫R2f(x)e-ix·kdx,and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\check{f}$$\end{document}fˇ denotes the inverse Fourier transform. We also consider the seminormIn the Hilbert case (p=2p=2) we will denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^s=W^{s,2}$$\end{document}Hs=Ws,2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{H}^s=\dot{W}^{s,2}$$\end{document}H˙s=W˙s,2 as usual. In general, we will consider s≥ 0s≥0, except when dealing with the Hamiltonian.

Proposition 2.2

Let (θ ,f)(θ,f) and (Θ ,F)(Θ,F) be linked by (2.1). Then, 2.5a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \theta (t)\Vert _{\dot{W}^{s,p}}&=(abt)^{\frac{\alpha +\frac{2}{p}-s}{a}-1} \Vert \Theta (\tau )\Vert _{\dot{W}^{s,p}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖θ(t)‖W˙s,p=(abt)α+2p-sa-1‖Θ(τ)‖W˙s,p,2.5b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f(t)\Vert _{\dot{W}^{s,p}}&=(abt)^{\frac{\alpha +\frac{2}{p}-s}{a}-2} \Vert F(\tau )\Vert _{\dot{W}^{s,p}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f(t)‖W˙s,p=(abt)α+2p-sa-2‖F(τ)‖W˙s,p.

Proof

Firstly, we write θ θ in the Fourier side,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\theta }(t,k)&=\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\theta (t,x)e^{-ix\cdot k}\,\textrm{d}x =(abt)^{\frac{\alpha }{a}-1}\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta (\tau ,X)e^{-ix\cdot k}\,\textrm{d}x\\&=(abt)^{\frac{2+\alpha }{a}-1}\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta (\tau ,X)e^{-iX\cdot K}\,\textrm{d}X =(abt)^{\frac{2+\alpha }{a}-1}\hat{\Theta }(\tau ,K), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ^(t,k)=∫R2θ(t,x)e-ix·kdx=(abt)αa-1∫R2Θ(τ,X)e-ix·kdx=(abt)2+αa-1∫R2Θ(τ,X)e-iX·KdX=(abt)2+αa-1Θ^(τ,K),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K=(abt)^{1/a}k. $$\end{document}K=(abt)1/ak. Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ g(t,k) =(abt)^{\frac{2+\alpha -s}{a}-1}G(\tau ,K), $$\end{document}g(t,k)=(abt)2+α-sa-1G(τ,K),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g=|k|^s\hat{\theta }$$\end{document}g=|k|sθ^ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=|K|^s\hat{\Theta }$$\end{document}G=|K|sΘ^. Definition 2.1

We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta _0,f)$$\end{document}(θ0,f) is a self-similar solution to the α α-SQG equation if the corresponding pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\bar{\Theta },F)$$\end{document}(Θ¯,F) given by the self-similar change of variables (2.1) is a stationary solution of the self-similar α α-SQG equation, that is,2.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{V}\cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta } -b\left( (a-\alpha )+X\cdot \nabla \right) \bar{\Theta } =F, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V¯·∇Θ¯-b(a-α)+X·∇Θ¯=F,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{V}$$\end{document}V¯ is recovered from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ through the α α-Biot-Savart law (1.2).

Remark 2.2

Observe that any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ becomes a solution by defining the force ad hoc as in equation (2.6). This degree of freedom is the main advantage of considering the forced equation, enabling the consideration of functions for which the stability analysis becomes feasible, although it still requires considerable effort.

In view of Proposition 2.2, the integrability conditions (1.5) and (1.7) for self-similar solutions require to take a in the regime\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 0<a<\alpha +\frac{2}{p}-s, $$\end{document}0<a<α+2p-s,which is possible if and only if s satisfies2.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} s<\alpha +\frac{2}{p}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}s<α+2p,as stated in Theorem 1.1. From now on, we consider these parameters to be fixed in this regime2.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0<a<\varepsilon \le \alpha +\frac{2}{p}-s, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0<a<ε≤α+2p-s,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\varepsilon <1+\alpha $$\end{document}0<ε<1+α is the parameter in Theorem 3.1.

Corollary 2.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta _0,f)$$\end{document}(θ0,f) be a self-similar solution with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\bar{\Theta },F)$$\end{document}(Θ¯,F) smooth and compactly supported. It holds that 2.9a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \theta _0(t)\Vert _{\dot{W}^{s,p}}&=(abt)^{\frac{\alpha +\frac{2}{p}-s}{a}-1} \Vert \bar{\Theta }\Vert _{\dot{W}^{s,p}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖θ0(t)‖W˙s,p=(abt)α+2p-sa-1‖Θ¯‖W˙s,p,2.9b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^t \Vert f(t')\Vert _{\dot{W}^{s,p}}\,\textrm{d}t'&=\frac{(abt)^{\frac{\alpha +\frac{2}{p}-s}{a}-1}}{ab\left( \frac{\alpha +\frac{2}{p}-s}{a}-1\right) } \Vert F\Vert _{\dot{W}^{s,p}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0t‖f(t′)‖W˙s,pdt′=(abt)α+2p-sa-1abα+2p-sa-1‖F‖W˙s,p. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _0|_{t=0}=0$$\end{document}θ0|t=0=0.

Proof

It follows by applying Definition 2.1 and Proposition 2.2. □ □

Linear Stability Equation

In this section we derive the linear stability equation around a (self-similar) solution. Given a background steady temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯, a perturbation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Theta _\epsilon =\bar{\Theta }+\epsilon \tilde{\Theta }, $$\end{document}Θϵ=Θ¯+ϵΘ~,is a second solution to the (self-similar) α α-SQG equ. (2.3) with the same initial data and force if and only if the deviation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ~ solves2.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\partial _\tau -L_b)\tilde{\Theta } +\epsilon (\tilde{V}\cdot \nabla \tilde{\Theta })=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(∂τ-Lb)Θ~+ϵ(V~·∇Θ~)=0,coupled with the initial condition2.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\Theta }|_{\tau =-\infty }=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ~|τ=-∞=0.The operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb is the linearization of the (self-similar) α α-SQG equation around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯. This can be decomposed into2.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_b =b(a-\alpha ) +T_b+K, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lb=b(a-α)+Tb+K,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_b\Theta&=(bX-\bar{V})\cdot \nabla \Theta ,\\ K\Theta&=-V\cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TbΘ=(bX-V¯)·∇Θ,KΘ=-V·∇Θ¯.The velocities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(V,\bar{V})$$\end{document}(V,V¯) are recovered from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Theta ,\bar{\Theta })$$\end{document}(Θ,Θ¯) respectively through the α α-Biot-Savart law (1.2).

Remark 2.3

Notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_b$$\end{document}Tb and K depend on a, α α and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯. Since the parameter a has already been fixed in (2.8), we omit this dependence. The dependence on α α and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ will be made explicit in the notation when necessary.

As usual in Stability theory, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ϵ→0 one is lead to study the linear equation2.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\partial _\tau -L_b)\Theta ^{\text {lin}}=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(∂τ-Lb)Θlin=0,and seek for solutions that grow exponentially in time, that is,2.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^{\text {lin}}(\tau ,X)=\Re (e^{\lambda \tau }W(X)), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θlin(τ,X)=ℜ(eλτW(X)),with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Re \lambda >0$$\end{document}ℜλ>0. For solutions of this form, equation (2.13) is equivalent to the eigenvalue problem2.15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_bW=\lambda W. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LbW=λW.In other words, linear instability translates into the existence of eigenvalues λ λ in the right half plane \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}_+$$\end{document}C+, and eigenfunctions W in some suitable Hilbert space, of the linear operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b=L_{b,\alpha ,\bar{\Theta }}$$\end{document}Lb=Lb,α,Θ¯.

An obvious but crucial observation is that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ e^{\lambda \tau } =t^{\frac{\lambda }{ab}} \rightarrow 0, $$\end{document}eλτ=tλab→0,as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}τ→-∞ (or equivalently t→ 0t→0), and therefore2.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^{\text {lin}}|_{\tau =-\infty }=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θlin|τ=-∞=0,which leads to non-uniqueness at the linear level.

Vortices

We consider the special case of radially symmetric temperatures, called vortices. In polar coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=Re^{i\phi }$$\end{document}X=Reiϕ, this corresponds to2.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{\Theta }(X) =\bar{\Theta }(R). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ¯(X)=Θ¯(R).In Lemma 2.1, we show that the corresponding velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{V}$$\end{document}V¯, recovered from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ through the α α-Biot-Savart law (1.2), has only an angular component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{V}_\phi $$\end{document}V¯ϕ. Moreover, we show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{V}_\phi $$\end{document}V¯ϕ is expressed in terms of the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{1,\alpha }$$\end{document}V1,α, defined below. We take the opportunity to introduce this operator more generally as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}Vn,α, since it will appear in the eigenspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_n$$\end{document}Un in the following subsection.

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}n∈Z, we define the operator2.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_{n,\alpha }[f](R) :=C_\alpha \int _0^\infty I_{n,\alpha }\left( \frac{R}{S}\right) f(S)S^{1-\alpha }\,\textrm{d}S, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vn,α[f](R):=Cα∫0∞In,αRSf(S)S1-αdS,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha $$\end{document}Cα is the constant in (1.2), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}In,α is the kernel2.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ) :=\frac{1}{\alpha }\int _{-\pi }^{\pi }\frac{\cos (n\beta )}{|\sigma -e^{i\beta }|^{\alpha }}\,\textrm{d}\beta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In,α(σ):=1α∫-ππcos(nβ)|σ-eiβ|αdβ,whenever these expressions make sense.

Remark 2.4

A priori, (2.19) seems ill defined for α =0α=0. However, an integration by parts (see (2.22)) shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}In,α can be expressed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ I_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ) =\frac{\sigma }{n}K_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ), $$\end{document}In,α(σ)=σnKn,α(σ),where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ) :=\int _{-\pi }^{\pi }\frac{\sin (\beta )\sin (n\beta )}{|\sigma -e^{i\beta }|^{2+\alpha }}\,\textrm{d}\beta , $$\end{document}Kn,α(σ):=∫-ππsin(β)sin(nβ)|σ-eiβ|2+αdβ,which is well defined for α =0α=0 as well. Since this case corresponds to the 2D Euler equation, which has already been analyzed in [31], we mostly deal with (2.19) for the sake of convenience. However, at certain points, it will be more convenient to use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}Kn,α.

Lemma 2.1

It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \bar{V}(X) =\bar{V}_\phi (R)e_\phi , \quad \text {with} \quad \bar{V}_\phi =- V_{1,\alpha }[\partial _R\bar{\Theta }], $$\end{document}V¯(X)=V¯ϕ(R)eϕ,withV¯ϕ=-V1,α[∂RΘ¯],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{1,\alpha }$$\end{document}V1,α is defined in (2.18).

Proof

By applying\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \nabla \bar{\Theta }(X) =\partial _R\bar{\Theta }(R)e^{i\phi }, $$\end{document}∇Θ¯(X)=∂RΘ¯(R)eiϕ,we compute (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =\phi -\varphi $$\end{document}β=ϕ-φ)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{V}(X) =-\frac{C_\alpha }{\alpha }\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\frac{\nabla ^\perp \bar{\Theta }(Y)}{|X-Y|^{\alpha }}\,\textrm{d}Y&=-\frac{C_\alpha }{\alpha }\int _{-\pi }^{\pi }\int _0^\infty \frac{ie^{i\varphi }\partial _R\bar{\Theta }(S)}{|Re^{i\phi }-Se^{i\varphi }|^\alpha }S\,\textrm{d}S\,\textrm{d}\varphi \\&=-ie^{i\phi }\frac{C_\alpha }{\alpha }\int _0^\infty \int _{-\pi }^{\pi } \frac{e^{-i\beta }}{|R{-}Se^{i\beta }|^\alpha }\,\textrm{d}\beta \partial _R\bar{\Theta }(S) S\,\textrm{d}S\\&=-ie^{i\phi }\frac{C_\alpha }{\alpha }\int _0^\infty \int _{-\pi }^{\pi } \frac{\cos \beta }{|R{-}Se^{i\beta }|^\alpha }\,\textrm{d}\beta \partial _R\bar{\Theta }(S) S\,\textrm{d}S. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V¯(X)=-Cαα∫R2∇⊥Θ¯(Y)|X-Y|αdY=-Cαα∫-ππ∫0∞ieiφ∂RΘ¯(S)|Reiϕ-Seiφ|αSdSdφ=-ieiϕCαα∫0∞∫-ππe-iβ|R-Seiβ|αdβ∂RΘ¯(S)SdS=-ieiϕCαα∫0∞∫-ππcosβ|R-Seiβ|αdβ∂RΘ¯(S)SdS.This concludes the proof. □ □

Corollary 2.2

It holds that (here V is recovered from W through the α α-Biot-Savart law (1.2))\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_bW&=\left( bR\partial _R-\frac{\bar{V}_\phi }{R}\partial _\phi \right) W,\\ KW&=-V_R\partial _R\bar{\Theta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TbW=bR∂R-V¯ϕR∂ϕW,KW=-VR∂RΘ¯.

Proof

On the one hand,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ X\cdot \nabla W=R\partial _R W. $$\end{document}X·∇W=R∂RW.On the other hand,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \bar{V}\cdot \nabla W=\frac{\bar{V}_\phi }{R}\partial _\phi W, \quad \quad V\cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta } =V_R\partial _R\bar{\Theta }, $$\end{document}V¯·∇W=V¯ϕR∂ϕW,V·∇Θ¯=VR∂RΘ¯,where we applied (2.17) and Lemma 2.1. □ □

Purely n-fold Symmetric Temperatures

In order to ensure that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ has finite Hamiltonian for α =0α=0, we will work in the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_0$$\end{document}U0 of vortices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}L2(R2) with zero-mean,2.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U_0 :=\left\{ \bar{\Theta }\in L^2 \,:\, \bar{\Theta }(X)=\bar{\Theta }(R)\, ,\ \int _0^\infty \bar{\Theta }(R)R\,\textrm{d}R=0\right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}U0:=Θ¯∈L2:Θ¯(X)=Θ¯(R),∫0∞Θ¯(R)RdR=0.Although the zero-mean condition is not strictly necessary for α >0α>0 for having finite Hamiltonian, it appears to be more convenient for producing unstable vortices (see Remark 3.3). In this context, given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\ne n\in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}0≠n∈Z, it is natural to seek eigenfunctions in the space of purely n-fold symmetric temperatures,2.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U_n:=\{W\in L^2\,:\,W(X)=W_n(R)e^{in\phi }\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Un:={W∈L2:W(X)=Wn(R)einϕ}.Notice that any element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_n$$\end{document}Un has zero-mean. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{-n}=U_n^*$$\end{document}U-n=Un∗, we will consider without loss of generality the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N.

Lemma 2.2

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in U_n$$\end{document}W∈Un,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ V_R(X) =in\frac{V_{n,\alpha }[W_n](R)}{R}e^{in\phi }, $$\end{document}VR(X)=inVn,α[Wn](R)Reinϕ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}Vn,α is defined in (2.18).

Proof

By writing the α α-Biot-Savart law (1.2) in polar coordinates (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =\phi -\varphi $$\end{document}β=ϕ-φ)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V(X)&=C_\alpha \int _0^\infty \int _{-\pi }^{\pi } \frac{(Re^{i\phi }-Se^{i\varphi })i}{|Re^{i\phi }-Se^{i\varphi }|^{2+\alpha }}W(Se^{i\varphi }) \,\textrm{d}\varphi S\,\textrm{d}S\\&=ie^{i\phi } C_\alpha \int _0^\infty \int _{-\pi }^{\pi } \frac{R-Se^{-i\beta }}{|R-Se^{i\beta }|^{2+\alpha }}W(Se^{i(\phi -\beta )}) \,\textrm{d}\beta S\,\textrm{d}S, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V(X)=Cα∫0∞∫-ππ(Reiϕ-Seiφ)i|Reiϕ-Seiφ|2+αW(Seiφ)dφSdS=ieiϕCα∫0∞∫-ππR-Se-iβ|R-Seiβ|2+αW(Sei(ϕ-β))dβSdS,we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_R =\Re (Ve^{-i\phi })&=-C_\alpha \Im \int _0^\infty \int _{-\pi }^{\pi } \frac{R-Se^{-i\beta }}{|R-Se^{i\beta }|^{2+\alpha }}W(Se^{i(\phi -\beta )}) \,\textrm{d}\beta S\,\textrm{d}S\\&=-C_\alpha \int _0^\infty \int _{-\pi }^{\pi } \frac{\sin \beta }{|R-Se^{i\beta }|^{2+\alpha }}W(Se^{i(\phi -\beta )})\,\textrm{d}\beta S^2\,\textrm{d}S. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VR=ℜ(Ve-iϕ)=-Cαℑ∫0∞∫-ππR-Se-iβ|R-Seiβ|2+αW(Sei(ϕ-β))dβSdS=-Cα∫0∞∫-ππsinβ|R-Seiβ|2+αW(Sei(ϕ-β))dβS2dS.We remark that this is the expression for the real operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_R$$\end{document}VR (accordingly for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$KW=-V_R\partial _R\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}KW=-VR∂RΘ¯). Next, we consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_R$$\end{document}VR acting on complex-valued W’s. Hence, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W(X)=W_n(R)e^{in\phi }$$\end{document}W(X)=Wn(R)einϕ, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_R&=-e^{in\phi }C_\alpha \int _0^\infty \int _{-\pi }^{\pi } \frac{\sin \beta e^{-in\beta }}{|R-Se^{i\beta }|^{2+\alpha }}\,\textrm{d}\beta W_n(S) S^2\,\textrm{d}S \\&=ie^{in\phi }C_\alpha \int _0^\infty K_{n,\alpha }\left( \frac{R}{S}\right) W_n(S)S^{-\alpha }\,\textrm{d}S, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VR=-einϕCα∫0∞∫-ππsinβe-inβ|R-Seiβ|2+αdβWn(S)S2dS=ieinϕCα∫0∞Kn,αRSWn(S)S-αdS,where2.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} K_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ) =\int _{-\pi }^{\pi }\frac{\sin \beta \sin (n\beta )}{|\sigma -e^{i\beta }|^{2+\alpha }}\,\textrm{d}\beta&=-\frac{1}{\alpha \sigma } \int _{-\pi }^{\pi }\partial _\beta \left( \frac{1}{|\sigma -e^{i\beta }|^{\alpha }}\right) \sin (n\beta )\,\textrm{d}\beta \\&=\frac{n}{\alpha \sigma } \int _{-\pi }^{\pi }\frac{\cos (n\beta )}{|\sigma -e^{i\beta }|^{\alpha }}\,\textrm{d}\beta =\frac{n}{\sigma }I_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Kn,α(σ)=∫-ππsinβsin(nβ)|σ-eiβ|2+αdβ=-1ασ∫-ππ∂β1|σ-eiβ|αsin(nβ)dβ=nασ∫-ππcos(nβ)|σ-eiβ|αdβ=nσIn,α(σ).This concludes the proof. □ □

Corollary 2.3

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in U_n$$\end{document}W∈Un, it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_bW&=\left( bR\partial _R-in\frac{\bar{V}_\phi }{R}\right) W_n e^{in\phi },\\ KW&=-in\frac{V_{n,\alpha }[W_n]}{R}\partial _R\bar{\Theta }e^{in\phi }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TbW=bR∂R-inV¯ϕRWneinϕ,KW=-inVn,α[Wn]R∂RΘ¯einϕ.

Proof

It follows from Corollary 2.2, definition (2.21) and Lemma 2.2. □ □

Therefore, the eigenvalue problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_bW=\lambda W$$\end{document}LbW=λW for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in U_n$$\end{document}W∈Un can be rewritten as2.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} b(a-\alpha )W_n +\left( bR\partial _R-in\frac{\bar{V}_\phi }{R}\right) W_n -in\partial _R\bar{\Theta }\frac{V_{n,\alpha }[W_n]}{R} =\lambda W_n, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}b(a-α)Wn+bR∂R-inV¯ϕRWn-in∂RΘ¯Vn,α[Wn]R=λWn,which we will refer to as the linear stability equation. We recall that the case b=0b=0 corresponds to the original coordinates, while b>0b>0 corresponds to the self-similar coordinates.

Remark 2.5

As mentioned, for b=0b=0, equation (2.23) corresponds to the stability equation in the original coordinates. In particular, for α =0α=0, this represents the Rayleigh stability equation for the 2D Euler equation at the vorticity level.

Definition 2.2

We say that the vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is unstable if, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\ne W\in U_n$$\end{document}0≠W∈Un satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{0,\bar{\Theta }}W=\lambda W$$\end{document}L0,Θ¯W=λW with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Re \lambda >0$$\end{document}ℜλ>0. Similarly, we say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is self-similarly unstable if, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N and b>0b>0, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\ne W\in U_n$$\end{document}0≠W∈Un satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{b,\bar{\Theta }}W=\lambda W$$\end{document}Lb,Θ¯W=λW with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Re \lambda >0$$\end{document}ℜλ>0.

Non-Linear Stability Equation

Let us assume that such a (self-similarly) unstable vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ exists. We decompose the deviation into\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \tilde{\Theta }=\Theta ^{\text {lin}} +\epsilon \Theta ^{\text {cor}}, $$\end{document}Θ~=Θlin+ϵΘcor,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^{\text {lin}}(\tau ,X)=\Re (e^{\lambda \tau }W(X))$$\end{document}Θlin(τ,X)=ℜ(eλτW(X)), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^{\text {cor}}$$\end{document}Θcor is the non-linear correction, to be determined. Namely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^{\text {cor}}$$\end{document}Θcor must satisfy the equation2.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\partial _\tau - L_b)\Theta ^{\text {cor}} +\underbrace{(V^{\text {lin}}+\epsilon V^{\text {cor}})\cdot \nabla (\Theta ^{\text {lin}}+\epsilon \Theta ^{\text {cor}})}_{\mathcal {F}} =0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(∂τ-Lb)Θcor+(Vlin+ϵVcor)·∇(Θlin+ϵΘcor)⏟F=0,which we will refer to as the non-linear stability equation, coupled with the initial condition2.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta ^{\text {cor}}|_{\tau =-\infty }=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θcor|τ=-∞=0.If we interpret \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F as a forcing term, since the linear part decays as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{\Re \lambda \tau }$$\end{document}eℜλτ and the contribution of the quadratic term is expected to be negligible for short times, one can naively expect to gain a slightly faster exponential decay by exploiting the Duhamel formula. We recall that the case b=0b=0 corresponds to the original coordinates, while b>0b>0 corresponds to the self-similar coordinates.

Definition 2.3

We say that the (self-similarly) unstable vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is also (self-similarly) non-linearly unstable if there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^{\text {cor}}$$\end{document}Θcor solving (2.24) and (2.25) with2.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Theta ^{\text {cor}}(\tau )\Vert _{L^2} =o(e^{\Re \lambda \tau }), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Θcor(τ)‖L2=o(eℜλτ),as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}τ→-∞.

Space of n-fold Symmetric Temperatures

In the previous sections we considered the linearization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb acting on the invariant subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_n$$\end{document}Un of purely n-fold symmetric temperatures. However, for the non-linear instability we will need to take into account the quadratic term, which introduces multiples of the original frequency n. For this reason, it is convenient to analyze \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb within the larger space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} Sketch of the Proof

In this section, we prove Theorem 1.2, as well as the following stronger version of Theorem 1.1.

Theorem 3.1

Remark 3.1

The improvement is that, in Theorem 3.1, the force and the solutions belong simultaneously to all the Sobolev spaces corresponding to the red region in Figure 1. The condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon < 1 + \alpha $$\end{document}ε<1+α will appear frequently in the proofs. Moreover, it guarantees that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in L^2$$\end{document}θ∈L2, which is sufficient to properly define the weak formulation at the level of θ θ.

Theorem 3.2

(Vishik’s non-uniqueness theorem) Let 2<p<∞ 2<p<∞. There exists a force3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f\in L^1([0,\infty ),\dot{H}^{-1}\cap L^1\cap L^p), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f∈L1([0,∞),H˙-1∩L1∩Lp),such that there are uncountably many solutions3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _\epsilon \in L^\infty ([0,\infty ),\dot{H}^{-1}\cap L^1\cap L^p), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θϵ∈L∞([0,∞),H˙-1∩L1∩Lp),to the 2D Euler equation starting from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^\circ =0$$\end{document}θ∘=0.

We begin with a general overview of the strategy. In short, the key observation is that a vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ that is self-similarly non-linearly unstable implies non-uniqueness. We first explain this idea succinctly and then outline the steps for constructing the vortex.

Firstly, we take the main solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _0$$\end{document}θ0 as the self-similar vortex corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯. According to Definition 2.1, this means that the pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta _0,f)$$\end{document}(θ0,f) is given by the self-similar change of variables 3.4a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _0(t,x)&=(abt)^{\frac{\alpha }{a}-1}\bar{\Theta }(X), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ0(t,x)=(abt)αa-1Θ¯(X),3.4b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(t,x)&=(abt)^{\frac{\alpha }{a}-2}F(X), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(t,x)=(abt)αa-2F(X), in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯. Remark 3.2

In Sect. 5, we choose a regularization parameter ɛ ε for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _R \bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}∂RΘ¯ scales as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^{-1}$$\end{document}ε-1. In Sect. 6, we show that the parameter b, which characterizes the separation rate of the nonunique solutions, can be chosen arbitrarily small. It is worth noting that making F small in (3.5) requires a trade-off between ɛ ε and b. This should be compared with the scenario in [2].

In Sect. 3.1, we rigorously prove Theorem 3.1 as a consequence of the properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^{\text {lin}}$$\end{document}Θlin, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^{\text {cor}}$$\end{document}Θcor, established in Theorems 3.4 and 3.5, along with the Sobolev scaling given in Proposition 2.2. Similarly, in Sect. 3.2, we prove Theorem 1.2 as a consequence of Theorems 3.3 and 3.5.

As introduced in Sect. 1.2, the proof of the existence of a self-similarly non-linearly unstable vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is divided into three main steps: 1. Eulerian, 2. Self-similar, and 3. Non-linear instability. Furthermore, Step 1. is further divided into two intermediate steps: 1.1. Construction of a piecewise constant unstable vortex and 1.2. Regularization.

Next, we outline these steps, which allow us to establish Theorems 3.3–3.5. The proofs of these theorems form the core of this work and will be presented rigorously in Sects. 4–7.

Step 1. Instability in the Eulerian coordinates

The first step involves constructing an unstable vortex that is smooth and compactly supported in the original coordinates. In other words, we must solve the stability equation (2.23) for b=0b=0. Since the construction of these vortices has its own physical and mathematical interest, and in order to maintain consistency in notation, we will adopt the convention of using lowercase letters until we return to the self-similar variables.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\theta }$$\end{document}θ¯ be a vortex with corresponding velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{v}$$\end{document}v¯. Theorem 3.3

For every n≥ 2n≥2, there exists an unstable vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }\in C_c^\infty \cap U_0$$\end{document}Θ¯∈Cc∞∩U0 such that the corresponding eigenfunction satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in C_c^\infty \cap U_n$$\end{document}W∈Cc∞∩Un.

Our proof of Theorem 3.3 is split into two steps.

Step 1.1. Piecewise constant unstable vortex

In Sect. 4 we construct an unstable vortex of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \bar{\theta } =c 1_{[0,r_1)} - 1_{[r_1,r_2)}, $$\end{document}θ¯=c1[0,r1)-1[r1,r2),in terms of some parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<r_1<r_2<\infty $$\end{document}0<r1<r2<∞ and c>0c>0, to be determined. For the sake of simplicity we fix\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ r_1=\sigma , \quad \quad r_2=1, $$\end{document}r1=σ,r2=1,for some 0<σ <10<σ<1, to be determined. Firstly, we choose c making the mean of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\theta }$$\end{document}θ¯ equal zero, and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\theta }\in U_0$$\end{document}θ¯∈U0. This is equivalent to3.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (1+c)\sigma ^2=1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(1+c)σ2=1.Secondly, since\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textrm{supp}(\partial _r\bar{\theta })=\{\sigma ,1\}, $$\end{document}supp(∂rθ¯)={σ,1},the linear stability equ.(3.7) turns into two conditions for the vector h=(h(σ ),h(1))h=(h(σ),h(1)). This can be written as a linear system3.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A h=zh, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ah=zh,where the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\in \mathbb {R}^{2\times 2}$$\end{document}A∈R2×2 depends on the parameter σ σ and the frequency n, namely3.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A= \left[ \begin{array}{cc} \sigma ^{-(2+\alpha )}J_{n,\alpha }(1)-\sigma ^{-1}I_{1,\alpha }(\sigma ) & \sigma ^{-1}I_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ) \\ -\sigma ^{-1} I_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ) & -J_{n,\alpha }(1) +\sigma ^{-1} I_{1,\alpha }(\sigma ) \end{array} \right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=σ-(2+α)Jn,α(1)-σ-1I1,α(σ)σ-1In,α(σ)-σ-1In,α(σ)-Jn,α(1)+σ-1I1,α(σ),with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{n,\alpha }=I_{1,\alpha }-I_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}Jn,α=I1,α-In,α. By linear algebra, there exists an eigenvector h≠ 0h≠0 with corresponding eigenvalue z if and only if z is a root of the characteristic polynomial \det (A-z)det(A-z).

For the 2D Euler equation (α =0α=0) the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial can be computed explicitly. This allows us to obtain the existence of a non-trivial h by an elementary explicit computation. The case α >0α>0 is considerably more intricate since the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial are not explicit, being given in terms of the integral \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}In,α evaluated at σ σ and 1. Furthermore, the situation is more delicate for SQG as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{n,1}$$\end{document}In,1 diverges at 1. Fortunately, in this case there is an extra cancellation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}Jn,α that allows us to consider not only SQG but also the complete regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <2$$\end{document}0≤α<2. In Sect. 4.1, we explicitly compute certain values and derive the precise asymptotics of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{n,\alpha }$$\end{document}In,α in terms of the Gamma function. These results allow us to show that the discriminant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (\sigma )$$\end{document}Δ(σ) of the characteristic polynomial satisfies (see Proposition 4.1)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Delta (1)=\Delta '(1)=0, \quad \quad \Delta ''(1)<0, $$\end{document}Δ(1)=Δ′(1)=0,Δ′′(1)<0,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <2$$\end{document}0≤α<2 and n≥ 2n≥2. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (\sigma )<0$$\end{document}Δ(σ)<0 for some 0<σ <10<σ<1, and thus ℑ z>0ℑz>0.

Remark 3.3

The zero-mean condition is imposed in the 2D Euler equations to ensure that the Hamiltonian remains finite. Although this condition is not strictly necessary for α > 0α>0, it can be shown that the discriminant for a general vortex satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta (1) \ge 0$$\end{document}Δ(1)≥0 and vanishes if and only if the vortex has zero mean. Therefore, to complete our argument, it is convenient to work within the class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_0$$\end{document}U0 for the full range of α α values.

Step 2. Instability in self-similar coordinates

In order to prove that the unstable vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is also self-similarly unstable, we follow Vishik’s argument. This requires decomposing the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb acting on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n^2$$\end{document}Ln2, as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L_b=A_b+C, $$\end{document}Lb=Ab+C,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(A_b)$$\end{document}(Ab) is a family of linear operators that generate contraction semigroups and possess certain continuity with respect to the parameter b, and C is compact (See Proposition 6.2). By classical operator theory, this implies that the spectrum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (L_b)$$\end{document}σ(Lb) satisfies that, for any b≥ 0b≥0 and w>0w>0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma (L_b)\cap \{\Re \lambda >w\} $$\end{document}σ(Lb)∩{ℜλ>w}is finite and consists of isolated eigenvalues. Now, Step 1 provides an eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _0$$\end{document}λ0 with positive real part for b=0b=0. Then, using the continuity with respect to the parameter b, it is possible to show that there must also be eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _b$$\end{document}λb (of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb) near \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _0$$\end{document}λ0 for sufficiently small b>0b>0.

For the 2D Euler equation (α =0α=0) this argument is applied to the decomposition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ A_b=b(a-\alpha ) + T_b, \quad \quad C=K, $$\end{document}Ab=b(a-α)+Tb,C=K,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_b$$\end{document}Tb and K are defined in (2.12). In this work, we check that K remains compact for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1 (see Lemma 6.5) and thus Vishik’s argument can be applied. For α =1α=1, the operator K is bounded but not compact. We bypass this obstacle by decomposing K into (see Proposition 6.3) K=S+C, K=S+C,where the operator S is skew-adjoint, thus preserving the growth of the semigroup, and the operator C is a commutator that can be shown to be compact (see Lemma 6.6). Therefore, we can apply Vishik’s argument to the decomposition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ A_b=b(a-\alpha ) + T_b +S, \quad \quad C=K-S. $$\end{document}Ab=b(a-α)+Tb+S,C=K-S.As a consequence, we obtain the following result for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1 (recall Definition 2.2):

Theorem 3.4

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}m∈N, there exist b>0b>0 and j≠ 0j≠0 such that the vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ from Theorem 3.3 is also self-similarly unstable and the corresponding eigenfunction satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in C_c^{m}\cap U_{jn}$$\end{document}W∈Ccm∩Ujn.

The properties of the eigenfunction are proved in Proposition 6.4. Indeed, this proposition states that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in C_c^{m+2}$$\end{document}W∈Ccm+2, as we address the possible loss of derivatives in the next step.

Step 3. Non-linear instability

The last step entails proving that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is also non-linearly unstable. In the remainder of this section, we discuss the strategy for proving (3.13). The proof starts by making some a priori energy estimates.
