To prove that the time of existence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _k$$\end{document}τk does not diverge to -∞ -∞ when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞, a bootstrapping argument is used in [2, 31, 129, 130]. As noted in the previous works, the bootstrapping argument does not work in Cartesian coordinates, but it does in polar coordinates, provided that the partial derivatives are estimated in the correct order. In this work, we realize that these inductive energy estimates can be extended to spaces with higher-order derivatives.

To this end, we need to express Cartesian derivatives in terms of polar coordinates. In fact, we use the relation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _X^K f =\sum _{0<|J|\le |K|}p_J^K\frac{\partial _R^{j_1}\partial _\phi ^{j_2}f}{R^{|K|-j_1}}, $$\end{document}∂XKf=∑0<|J|≤|K|pJK∂R1∂ϕ2fR|K|-j1,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_J^K=p_J^K(\cos \phi ,\sin \phi )$$\end{document}pJK=pJK(cosϕ,sinϕ) are polynomials (see Appendix A.2). These partial derivatives need to be estimated in the following order\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{array}{cccc} \displaystyle \frac{\partial _\phi }{R^m} & \displaystyle \frac{\partial _R}{R^{m-1}} & & \\ \displaystyle \frac{\partial _\phi ^2}{R^m} & \displaystyle \frac{\partial _R\partial _\phi }{R^{m-1}} & \displaystyle \frac{\partial _R^2}{R^{m-2}} & \\ \displaystyle \frac{\partial _\phi ^3}{R^m} & \displaystyle \frac{\partial _R\partial _\phi ^2}{R^{m-1}} & \displaystyle \frac{\partial _R^2\partial _\phi }{R^{m-2}} & \displaystyle \frac{\partial _R^3}{R^{m-3}} \\ \cdots \end{array} $$\end{document}∂ϕRm∂RRm-1∂ϕ2Rm∂R∂ϕRm-1∂R2Rm-2∂ϕ3Rm∂R∂ϕ2Rm-1∂R2∂ϕRm-2∂R3Rm-3⋯This suggests to consider the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^m\subset L_n^2$$\end{document}Ym⊂Ln2 endowed with the norm\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert f\Vert _{Y^m} :=\Vert f\omega \Vert _{L^2} +\sum _{0<|J|\le m}\left\| \frac{\partial $$\end{document}‖f‖Zm:=‖fω‖L2+∑0<|K|≤m∂XKfRm-|K|ωL2.Notice that, although \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \begin{document}$$Y^m\subsetneq H^m_\omega $$\end{document}Ym⊊Hωm for m>1+α m>1+α due to the weight at the origin (see Lemma 7.4).

In the previous works for the 2D Euler equation [2, 31, 129, 130], the energy estimates were conducted for the global smooth solution starting at time τ =-kτ=-k. However, for α >0α>0 the lack of global well-posedness makes it necessary to regularize the system in order to justify the energy estimates. Furthermore, standard regularization using mollifiers does not fit well with the weighted energy space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^m$$\end{document}Ym. Instead, we used the regularization that comes from linearizing the equation. We consider the following linearized system. Starting from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _k^{(0)}=0$$\end{document}Θk(0)=0, we define recursively \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _k^{(q)}$$\end{document}Θk(q), for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}q∈N, as the solution to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\partial _\tau - L_b)\Theta _k^{(q)} +\underbrace{(V^{\text {lin}}+\epsilon V_k^{(q-1)})\cdot \nabla (\Theta ^{\text {lin}}+\epsilon \Theta _k^{(q)})}_{\mathcal {F}_k^{(q)}} =0. $$\end{document}(∂τ-Lb)Θk(q)+(Vlin+ϵVk(q-1))·∇(Θlin+ϵΘk(q))⏟Fk(q)=0.This iteration defines a sequence of global solutions, which can be shown to lie in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^{m+1}$$\end{document}Ym+1 (see Proposition 7.1). Then, we apply the inductive energy estimates on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^m$$\end{document}Ym to gain extra decay (see Proposition 7.3). With this, we deduce that the time of existence in our iteration scheme does not collapse (see Corollary 7.1). Finally, by a standard argument, we verify that the sequence converges to a solution as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}q→∞ and later \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞. We obtain the following result (recall Definition 2.3):

Theorem 3.5

The vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ from Theorem 3.3 (respectively Theorem 3.4) is also (self-similarly) non-linearly unstable. Moreover, there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _0>0$$\end{document}δ0>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\tau }>-\infty $$\end{document}τ¯>-∞ such that the correcting term is n-fold symmetric and satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^\text {cor}\in C((-\infty ,\bar{\tau }],H_{\omega }^m\cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}})$$\end{document}Θcor∈C((-∞,τ¯],Hωm∩H˙α-22) with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \Theta ^{\text {cor}}(\tau )\Vert _{H_\omega ^m}\le e^{(1+\delta _0)\Re \lambda \tau } $$\end{document}‖Θcor(τ)‖Hωm≤e(1+δ0)ℜλτfor all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\infty <\tau \le \bar{\tau }$$\end{document}-∞<τ≤τ¯.

Proof of Theorem 1.2

We consider the n-fold symmetric temperature\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta =\bar{\Theta }+\Theta ^{\text {lin}}+\Theta ^{\text {cor}} \in C((-\infty ,\bar{\tau }],H^m\cap \dot{H}^{\frac{\alpha -2}{2}}),$$\end{document}θ=Θ¯+Θlin+Θcor∈C((-∞,τ¯],Hm∩H˙α-22),as obtained from Theorems 3.3 and 3.5. Here we have b=0b=0, and thus f=0f=0. It follows that θ θ satisfies the conditions in Theorem 1.2. By shifting the time interval, we can assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\tau }=0$$\end{document}τ¯=0.

The Golovkin Trick

In a recent preprint [61], Dolce and Mescolini revived a clever trick from Golovkin [82] which allows proving non-uniqueness once self-similar instability is established at the linear level. As the authors point out, the trick appears to work for quadratic PDEs. Here, we revisit this trick in the context of the α α-SQG equation for the reader’s convenience.

Once Steps (1)-(3) have been completed, we have a self-similarly unstable vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }\in C_c^\infty $$\end{document}Θ¯∈Cc∞ and the corresponding eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathbb {C}_+$$\end{document}λ∈C+ and eigenfunction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\in C_c^m$$\end{document}W∈Ccm solving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b W=\lambda W$$\end{document}LbW=λW, with which we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ^{\text {lin}}=\Re (e^{\lambda \tau }W)$$\end{document}Θlin=ℜ(eλτW). Then, we consider the two temperatures3.15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Theta _\pm =\bar{\Theta }\pm \Theta ^{\text {lin}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Θ±=Θ¯±Θlin.It is straightforward to check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _+$$\end{document}Θ+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _-$$\end{document}Θ- solve the (self-similar) α α-SQG equation (2.3) with the same initial condition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Theta _{\pm }|_{\tau =-\infty }=\bar{\Theta }, $$\end{document}Θ±|τ=-∞=Θ¯,and the Golovkin force\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ G=F+V^{\text {lin}}\cdot \nabla \Theta ^{\text {lin}}, $$\end{document}G=F+Vlin·∇Θlin,where F is the Vishik force (3.5). The smoothness of the solutions allows us to conclude Theorem 3.1 through the Sobolev scaling (Proposition 2.2). Vishik’s and Golovkin’s forces differ in several aspects. On the one hand, F is radially symmetric and self-similar, while G is n-fold symmetric and depends on τ τ due to the quadratic term. On the other hand, F=0F=0 for b=0b=0, while G is not.

Piecewise Constant Unstable Vortex

In this section we construct a piecewise constant unstable vortex. Lemma 4.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1. There exists C>0C>0 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |I_{n,\alpha }'(\sigma )| \le \frac{C}{|1-\sigma |^\alpha }, $$\end{document}|In,α′(σ)|≤C|1-σ|α,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<|\sigma -1|\le \frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}0<|σ-1|≤12. Therefore,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |I_{n,\alpha }(\sigma )-I_{n,\alpha }(1)| \le C\frac{|1-\sigma |^{1-\alpha }}{1-\alpha }. $$\end{document}|In,α(σ)-In,α(1)|≤C|1-σ|1-α1-α.

Proof

For 0<α <10<α<1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<|\sigma -1|\le \frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}0<|σ-1|≤12, we can differentiate under the integral sign\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ I_{n,\alpha }'(\sigma ) =-\int _{-\pi }^{\pi }\frac{\sigma -\cos \beta }{|\sigma -e^{i\beta }|^{2+\alpha }}\cos (n\beta )\,\textrm{d}\beta . $$\end{document}In,α′(σ)=-∫-ππσ-cosβ|σ-eiβ|2+αcos(nβ)dβ.This can be bounded by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |I_{n,\alpha }' (\sigma )|&\le 2 \int _{0}^{\pi }\frac{1}{|\sigma -e^{i\beta }|^{1+\alpha }}\,\textrm{d}\beta = 4\int _{0}^{\pi /2} \frac{1}{\left( (1-\sigma )^2+\sigma (2\sin \beta )^2\right) ^{\frac{1+\alpha }{2}}}\,\textrm{d}\beta \\&= 4\int _{0}^{\pi /2} \frac{1-\cos \beta }{\left( (1-\sigma )^2+\sigma (2\sin \beta )^2\right) ^{\frac{1+\alpha }{2}}}\,\textrm{d}\beta \\&\quad +4\int _{0}^{\pi /2} \frac{\cos \beta }{\left( (1-\sigma )^2+\sigma (2\sin \beta )^2\right) ^{\frac{1+\alpha }{2}}}\,\textrm{d}\beta =A+B. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|In,α′(σ)|≤2∫0π1|σ-eiβ|1+αdβ=4∫0π/21(1-σ)2+σ(2sinβ)21+α2dβ=4∫0π/21-cosβ(1-σ)2+σ(2sinβ)21+α2dβ+4∫0π/2cosβ(1-σ)2+σ(2sinβ)21+α2dβ=A+B.The first term A can be bounded easily. For the bad term B, by making the changes of variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=\sin \beta $$\end{document}u=sinβ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w=\frac{2\sqrt{\sigma }}{|1-\sigma |}u$$\end{document}w=2σ|1-σ|u, we estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B&=4\int _{0}^{1} \frac{\,\textrm{d}u}{\left( (1-\sigma )^2+(2\sigma u)^2\right) ^{\frac{1+\alpha }{2}}} =\frac{2}{\sigma |1-\sigma |^\alpha }\int _{0}^{\frac{2\sqrt{\sigma }}{|1-\sigma |}} \frac{\,\textrm{d}w}{\left( 1+w^2\right) ^{\frac{1+\alpha }{2}}}\\&\le \frac{4}{|1-\sigma |^\alpha }\int _{0}^{\infty } \frac{\,\textrm{d}w}{\left( 1+w^2\right) ^{\frac{1+\alpha }{2}}} \le \frac{C}{\alpha |1-\sigma |^\alpha }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}B=4∫01du(1-σ)2+(2σu)21+α2=2σ|1-σ|α∫02σ|1-σ|dw1+w21+α2≤4|1-σ|α∫0∞dw1+w21+α2≤Cα|1-σ|α.Notice that for α =0α=0 the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{n,0}(\sigma )$$\end{document}In,0(σ) is Lipschitz at σ =1σ=1 (see (4.3)). Finally, we apply the fundamental theorem of calculus. □ □

The next lemma was proven in [27, Lemma 4.12]. Here, we present a more direct proof for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 4.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N. There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R\in C^1$$\end{document}R∈C1 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ I_{n,1}(\sigma )=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\sigma }}\log |1-\sigma |+R(\sigma ) $$\end{document}In,1(σ)=-2σlog|1-σ|+R(σ)for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<|\sigma -1|\le \frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}0<|σ-1|≤12.

Proof

We split\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_{n,1}(\sigma )&=4\int _0^{\pi /2} \frac{\cos (2n\beta )}{\sqrt{(1-\sigma )^2+\sigma (2\sin \beta )^2}}\,\textrm{d}\beta \\&=4\int _0^{\pi /2} \frac{\cos (2n\beta )-\cos \beta }{\sqrt{(1-\sigma )^2+\sigma (2\sin \beta )^2}}\,\textrm{d}\beta \\&\quad +4\int _0^{\pi /2} \frac{\cos \beta }{\sqrt{(1-\sigma )^2+\sigma (2\sin \beta )^2}}\,\textrm{d}\beta =A+B. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In,1(σ)=4∫0π/2cos(2nβ)(1-σ)2+σ(2sinβ)2dβ=4∫0π/2cos(2nβ)-cosβ(1-σ)2+σ(2sinβ)2dβ+4∫0π/2cosβ(1-σ)2+σ(2sinβ)2dβ=A+B.By the dominated convergence theorem, it is easy to check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\in C^1$$\end{document}A∈C1. For the bad term B, by making the changes of variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=\sin \beta $$\end{document}u=sinβ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w=\frac{2\sqrt{\sigma }}{|1-\sigma |}u$$\end{document}w=2σ|1-σ|u, we compute that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\sigma }}\int _0^{\frac{2\sqrt{\sigma }}{|1-\sigma |}} \frac{\,\textrm{d}w}{\sqrt{1+w^2}} =\frac{2}{\sqrt{\sigma }}\textrm{arcsinh}\left( \frac{2\sqrt{\sigma }}{|1-\sigma |}\right) =-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\sigma }}\log |1-\sigma |+C, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}B=2σ∫02σ|1-σ|dw1+w2=2σarcsinh2σ|1-σ|=-2σlog|1-σ|+C,where C is a smooth function. In the last equality we have used the formula\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{arcsinh}(x)=\log (x+\sqrt{x^2+1}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}arcsinh(x)=log(x+x2+1),which implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\sigma }} \log (2\sqrt{\sigma } +\sqrt{4\sigma +(1-\sigma )^2}). $$\end{document}C=2σlog(2σ+4σ+(1-σ)2).Finally, we take R=A+CR=A+C. □ □

The rest of this section will be devoted to analyzing the operator4.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J_{n,\alpha }(\sigma ) =(I_{1,\alpha }-I_{n,\alpha })(\sigma ) =\frac{1}{\alpha }\int _{-\pi }^{\pi }\frac{\cos (\beta )-\cos (n\beta )}{|\sigma -e^{i\beta }|^{\alpha }}\,\textrm{d}\beta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jn,α(σ)=(I1,α-In,α)(σ)=1α∫-ππcos(β)-cos(nβ)|σ-eiβ|αdβ,which is now well defined at σ =1σ=1 for α <3α<3 thanks to the extra cancellation in the numerator.

Lemma 4.4

$$\end{document}Fn(α)<1,while for 1<α <31<α<3, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_k(1)=1$$\end{document}fk(1)=1 for all k=0,… ,n-1k=0,…,n-1, and thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} Lemma 4.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N. \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _1>\sigma _0$$\end{document}σ1>σ0. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L_{n,\alpha }''(1) =2J_{n,\alpha }''(1) -(1+\alpha )(2+\alpha )J_{n,\alpha }(1) {=}-2\alpha J_{n,2+\alpha }(1) -\frac{(2+\alpha )^2}{2}J_{n,\alpha }(1){<}0. $$\end{document}Ln,α′′(1)=2Jn,α′′(1)-(1+α)(2+α)Jn,α(1)=-2αJn,2+α(1)-(2+α)22Jn,α(1)<0.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le \alpha <2$$\end{document}1≤α<2, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lim _{\sigma \rightarrow 1}L_{n,\alpha }''(\sigma )=-\infty . $$\end{document}limσ→1Ln,α′′(σ)=-∞.This concludes the proof. □ □

Regularization

In this section we prove Theorem 3.3. To this end, we show that there is a regularization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\theta }^\varepsilon $$\end{document}θ¯ε, for some small ɛ >0ε>0, of the piecewise constant vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\theta }$$\end{document}θ¯ that we constructed in the previous section, that is also unstable. As we explained in Sect. 3, it is convenient to regularize \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\theta }$$\end{document}θ¯ in such a way that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\theta }^\varepsilon $$\end{document}θ¯ε also has zero mean. To this end, we take a mollifier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in C_c^\infty (I)$$\end{document}η∈Cc∞(I) with I=(-1,1)I=(-1,1) satisfying the properties of the following lemma:

Lemma 5.1

There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in C_c^\infty (-1,1)$$\end{document}η∈Cc∞(-1,1) such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{-1}^{1}\eta (\rho )\,\textrm{d}\rho =1, \quad \quad \int _{-1}^{1}\eta (\rho )\rho \,\textrm{d}\rho =\int _{-1}^{1}\eta (\rho )\rho ^2\,\textrm{d}\rho =0. $$\end{document}∫-11η(ρ)dρ=1,∫-11η(ρ)ρdρ=∫-11η(ρ)ρ2dρ=0.

Rescaling

In this section we zoom into each interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\varepsilon (r_j)$$\end{document}Bε(rj) through the change of variables\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ r=r_j+\varepsilon \rho , $$\end{document}r=rj+ερ,for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho \in I=(-1,1)$$\end{document}ρ∈I=(-1,1) and j=1,2j=1,2. From now on we will denote that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ c_1=-(1+c), \quad \quad c_2=1, $$\end{document}c1=-(1+c),c2=1,order to make the notation more compact. In the next lemma, we use the conditions on the momenta of η η.

Lemma 5.2

(Rescaling of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\theta }^\varepsilon $$\end{document}θ¯ε) It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \varepsilon \partial _r\bar{\theta }^\varepsilon (r) =c_j\eta (\rho ), $$\end{document}ε∂rθ¯ε(r)=cjη(ρ),for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r=r_j+\varepsilon \rho $$\end{document}r=rj+ερ. Proof

By differentiating (5.1), we obtain the first claim,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \varepsilon \partial _r\bar{\theta }^\varepsilon =\varepsilon (c_1\delta _{r_1} +c_2\delta _{r_2})*\eta ^\varepsilon =c_1 \eta \left( \frac{r-r_1}{\varepsilon }\right) +c_2 \eta \left( \frac{r-r_2}{\varepsilon }\right) . $$\end{document}ε∂rθ¯ε=ε(c1δr1+c2δr2)∗ηε=c1ηr-r1ε+c2ηr-r2ε.Hence, by applying Lemma 5.1, we deduce that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \int _0^\infty \bar{\theta }^\varepsilon r\,\textrm{d}r =-\frac{1}{2}\int _0^\infty \partial _r\bar{\theta }^\varepsilon r^2\,\textrm{d}r\\ & =-\frac{1}{2} \sum _{j=1,2}c_j \int _{-1}^{1} \eta (\rho )(r_j+\varepsilon \rho )^2\,\textrm{d}\rho =-\frac{1}{2} \sum _{j=1,2}c_j r_j^2=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0∞θ¯εrdr=-12∫0∞∂rθ¯εr2dr=-12∑j=1,2cj∫-11η(ρ)(rj+ερ)2dρ=-12∑j=1,2cjrj2=0,where the last equality follows from (4.14). We expand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^\varepsilon $$\end{document}hε in each interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_\varepsilon (r_j)$$\end{document}Bε(rj) as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ h^\varepsilon (r) =h_j+\varepsilon g_j(\rho ), $$\end{document}hε(r)=hj+εgj(ρ),for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r=r_j+\varepsilon \rho $$\end{document}r=rj+ερ, and for some profiles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_j\in L^2(I)$$\end{document}gj∈L2(I) to be determined. As a corollary, we obtain a simplification of the linear stability equation.

Lemma 5.3

(Expansion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_0^\varepsilon $$\end{document}A0ε) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1. Corollary 5.1

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha \le 1$$\end{document}0≤α≤1, the linear stability equation (5.3) can be rewritten as5.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (A^\varepsilon -z)g =(y-B_0)h+ \varepsilon yg. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Aε-z)g=(y-B0)h+εyg.

Lemma 5.4

(Expansion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_1^\varepsilon $$\end{document}A1ε) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1. Corollary 5.2

Inverting the Linear Operator

Notice that we can split\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A=A_0+A_1=D+C, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=A0+A1=D+C,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (Df)_j(\rho )= & -f_j(\rho )\sum _{k=1,2} c_k\frac{r_k^{1-\alpha }}{r_j} I_{1,\alpha }\left( \frac{r_j}{r_k}\right) ,\\ (Cf)_j= & \sum _{k=1,2} c_k\frac{r_k^{1-\alpha }}{r_j} I_{n,\alpha }\left( \frac{r_j}{r_k}\right) \int _{-1}^{1}f_k(\varrho )\eta (\varrho )\,\textrm{d}\varrho . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Df)j(ρ)=-fj(ρ)∑k=1,2ckrk1-αrjI1,αrjrk,(Cf)j=∑k=1,2ckrk1-αrjIn,αrjrk∫-11fk(ϱ)η(ϱ)dϱ.Notice that D is a diagonal operator, and therefore (D-z)(D-z) is invertible, and that C is constant valued, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C:L^2(I)^2\rightarrow \mathbb {C}^2$$\end{document}C:L2(I)2→C2. The next lemma takes advantage of this structure to reformulate the linear stability equation in order to find the corresponding eigenvalue with positive imaginary part. The proof is analogous to [31, Lemma 4.4]. Since it is brief, we recall it here for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 5.5

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Since δ δ is arbitrary, we will take δ =0δ=0 for simplicity.

Fixed Point Argument

For ɛ =0ε=0 the equation (5.10) is explicit for (g, y) and thus we can find a solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(g^0,y^0)$$\end{document}(g0,y0). For small ɛ >0ε>0 we will apply a fixed point argument. The proof is again analogous to [31, Proposition 4.5].

Proposition 5.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1. For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M>\Vert (g^0,y^0)\Vert _{L^2(I^2)\times \mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}M>‖(g0,y0)‖L2(I2)×C there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _0>0$$\end{document}ε0>0 satisfying that: for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \varepsilon \le \varepsilon _0$$\end{document}0≤ε≤ε0 there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(g^\varepsilon ,y^\varepsilon )\in L^2(I)^2\times \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}(gε,yε)∈L2(I)2×C solving (5.10) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert (g^\varepsilon ,y^\varepsilon )\Vert _{L^2(I)^2\times \mathbb {C}}\le M$$\end{document}‖(gε,yε)‖L2(I)2×C≤M. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Im z^\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ℑzε>0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon <\frac{\Im z}{M}$$\end{document}ε<ℑzM.

Proof

We fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1. Let us denote by F the map on the right-hand side of (5.10), that is, we rewrite this equation compactly as (g,y)=F(ɛ ,g,y). (g,y)=F(ε,g,y).For ɛ =0ε=0, the functional F is constant, namely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(0,g,y)=(g^0,y^0)$$\end{document}F(0,g,y)=(g0,y0). Since all the operators involved in F are bounded, depend continuously on ɛ ε at zero, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert (g^0,y^0)\Vert _{L^2(I)^2\times \mathbb {C}}<M$$\end{document}‖(g0,y0)‖L2(I)2×C<M, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _0>0$$\end{document}ε0>0 such that F maps the ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_M$$\end{document}BM of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(I)^2\times \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}L2(I)2×C into itself. Moreover, we can choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _0>0$$\end{document}ε0>0 such that F becomes a contraction on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_M$$\end{document}BM. Then, we can apply the classical Banach fixed point theorem to find our required solution. □ □

Lemma 5.8

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}i∈N,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ R^i\partial ^i_R\left( \frac{\bar{V}_\phi }{R}\right) \in L^\infty . $$\end{document}Ri∂RiV¯ϕR∈L∞.

Proof

The next proposition will be applied in the proof of Theorem 3.5. Remark 5.1

In the next section, we prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is unstable in self-similar coordinates using Vishik’s spectral argument and semigroup theory. This approach could be avoided if one were able to solve the α α-Rayleigh stability equation (2.23) directly for b>0b>0. Given the simplicity of our vortex, this seems approachable but it could require substantial additional work. We are grateful to the anonymous referee for this interesting observation.

Self-Similar Instability

In this section we prove Theorem 3.4, and provide growth bounds of the semigroup generated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb. From now on we consider n≥ 2n≥2 fixed.

The Linear Operators

We split the linearization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} We recall that in Sect. 2.2, we verified that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{jn}$$\end{document}Ujn is invariant under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_b$$\end{document}Tb and K for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}j∈Z. The reason for considering the direct sum\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_n^2=\bigoplus _{j\in \mathbb {Z}}U_{jn} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ln2=⨁j∈ZUjnis that the quadratic term in the α α-SQG equation lacks invariance in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{jn}$$\end{document}Ujn, but it is invariant in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n^2$$\end{document}Ln2. The invariance in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n^2$$\end{document}Ln2 follows from the following identity (see (2.27) and (6.6))\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textrm{div}(V\Theta )(X) =\textrm{div}\left( e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{n}}(V\Theta )(e^{\frac{2\pi i}{n}} X)\right) =\textrm{div}(V\Theta )(e^{\frac{2\pi i}{n}} X). $$\end{document}div(VΘ)(X)=dive-2πin(VΘ)(e2πinX)=div(VΘ)(e2πinX).More specifically, in Sect. 7 we will need the growth bound of the semigroup generated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb acting on the full space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n^2$$\end{document}Ln2.

As we mentioned in the introduction, one of the key points in Vishik’s spectral analysis [129, 130] (as well as in [2, 31]) for the 2D Euler equation (α =0α=0) is the fact that K is compact. This still holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1, but no longer for α =1α=1 (see Sect. 6.4). In Sect. 6.6, we show how to overcome this difficulty by appropriately decomposing the operator K.

Spectral Analysis

In this section, we present the part of the analysis that is common to both cases: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \alpha < 1$$\end{document}0≤α<1 and α = 1α=1. For this purpose, we recall some classical results in Operator theory that will be useful during the analysis. We consider linear operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:D(A)\subset H\rightarrow H$$\end{document}A:D(A)⊂H→H acting on some Hilbert space H, where D(A) is the domain of A. For a fixed H, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K the space of bounded and compact operators, respectively. In the next sections we will consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=L_n^2$$\end{document}H=Ln2.

Firstly, we recall the stability of strongly continuous semigroups under bounded perturbations, which can be found in [68, Chapter III, Bounded Perturbation Theorem].

Proposition 6.1

Let A be a linear operator on H generating a strongly continuous semigroup, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\in \mathcal {L}.$$\end{document}B∈L. Then, A+BA+B generates a strongly continuous semigroup.

For the proof of Theorem 3.4, we will decompose the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_b$$\end{document}Lb into an operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_b$$\end{document}Ab that satisfies a series of properties detailed in the proposition below, and a compact operator C. The proof of this proposition can be found in Appendix A.1 and follows the same argument as in [2, 31]. We recall it here for the convenience of the reader. We remark that although this proposition could be further generalized, we choose to present the version that is most useful for our purposes. See Appendix A.1 and [68] for more details on operator theory and definitions. In particular, see Definition A.1 for the notion of contraction semigroup and (A.1) for the definition of the growth bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _0$$\end{document}ω0.

The Transport Operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_b$$\end{document}Tb

Lemma 6.1

Notice that the velocity field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{V}_b=\bar{V}-bX$$\end{document}V¯b=V¯-bX is Lipschitz. \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _\tau J_{\bar{X}_b}=\textrm{div}(\bar{V}_b)J_{\bar{X}_b} \quad \text {with}\quad \textrm{div}(\bar{V}_b)=-2b, Lemma 6.3

Case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1

Lemma 6.4

The operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_b=b(a-\alpha )+T_b$$\end{document}Ab=b(a-α)+Tb satisfy the condition (1) in Proposition 6.2.

Proof

\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta T_b\Theta \,\textrm{d}X =-\frac{1}{2}\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\bar{V}_b\cdot \nabla |\Theta |^2\,\textrm{d}X =-b\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}|\Theta |^2\,\textrm{d}X, $$\end{document}∫R2ΘTbΘdX=-12∫R2V¯b·∇|Θ|2dX=-b∫R2|Θ|2dX,the following energy estimate shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_b=b(a-\alpha )+T_b$$\end{document}Ab=b(a-α)+Tb is a contraction\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2}\partial _\tau \int _{\mathbb {R}^2}|\Theta |^2\,\textrm{d}X =\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta A_b\Theta \,\textrm{d}X = b(a-\alpha -1)\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}|\Theta |^2\,\textrm{d}X\le 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}12∂τ∫R2|Θ|2dX=∫R2ΘAbΘdX=b(a-α-1)∫R2|Θ|2dX≤0.In the last inequality we use our choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<a<\varepsilon <1+\alpha $$\end{document}0<a<ε<1+α (recall (2.8)). We remark that the same inequality is obtained in the full domain by density. The continuity in b follows analogously to that of the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_b$$\end{document}Tb in the proof of Lemma 6.1. □ □

Secondly, we check that C=KC=K satisfies condition (2) in Proposition 6.2.

Lemma 6.5

The operator K is compact in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n^2$$\end{document}Ln2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \alpha <1$$\end{document}0≤α<1.

Proof

It follows by applying Lemma 6.2 and the (fractional) Rellich-Kondrachov Theorem (see e.g. [58, Sect. 7]). Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K\Theta =-V\cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}KΘ=-V·∇Θ¯ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ smooth and compactly supported. □ □

Finally, notice that condition (3) in Proposition 6.2 was proved in Theorem 3.3. Since we have verified conditions (1)-(3), Proposition 6.2 implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is self-similarly unstable.

Case α =1α=1

In this section, we prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Theta }$$\end{document}Θ¯ is self-similarly unstable for SQG. In this case we cannot apply Proposition 6.2 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_b=b(a-\alpha )+T_b$$\end{document}Ab=b(a-α)+Tb since K is not compact for α =1α=1. We bypass this obstacle by decomposing K into a skew-adjoint operator S, which preserves the growth bound of the semigroup, along with a commutator C, which turns out to be compact.

Proposition 6.3

It holds that K=S+C, K=S+C,where S is a skew-adjoint operator, and C is the commutator6.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C\Theta =\frac{1}{2}[\nabla \bar{\Theta },\Lambda ^{-1}\nabla ^\perp ]\Theta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CΘ=12[∇Θ¯,Λ-1∇⊥]Θ.

Proof

This follows by integrating by parts:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta K\Theta \,\textrm{d}X&=\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta (\nabla ^\perp \Lambda ^{-1}\Theta )\cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta }\,\textrm{d}X\\&=-\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Lambda ^{-1}\Theta (\nabla ^\perp \Theta \cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta })\,\textrm{d}X\\&=-\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta \Lambda ^{-1}(\nabla ^\perp \Theta \cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta })\,\textrm{d}X. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫R2ΘKΘdX=∫R2Θ(∇⊥Λ-1Θ)·∇Θ¯dX=-∫R2Λ-1Θ(∇⊥Θ·∇Θ¯)dX=-∫R2ΘΛ-1(∇⊥Θ·∇Θ¯)dX.Therefore,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta K\Theta \,\textrm{d}X =\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta C\Theta \,\textrm{d}X, $$\end{document}∫R2ΘKΘdX=∫R2ΘCΘdX,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C\Theta =\frac{1}{2}((\Lambda ^{-1}\nabla ^\perp \Theta )\cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta } -\Lambda ^{-1}(\nabla ^\perp \Theta \cdot \nabla \bar{\Theta })). $$\end{document}CΘ=12((Λ-1∇⊥Θ)·∇Θ¯-Λ-1(∇⊥Θ·∇Θ¯)).By definition, we have6.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta S\Theta \,\textrm{d}X =\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\Theta (K-C)\Theta \,\textrm{d}X=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫R2ΘSΘdX=∫R2Θ(K-C)ΘdX=0.This property is equivalent to being skew-adjoint. □ □

We will apply Proposition 6.2 to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ A_b =b(a-\alpha )+T_b+S, \quad \quad C=K-S. $$\end{document}Ab=b(a-α)+Tb+S,C=K-S.

Lemma 6.6

The commutator (6.8) is compact in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_n^2$$\end{document}Ln2.

Proof

It is well known that C is a smoothing operator in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 (see e.g. [123]). Therefore, it is compact when restricted to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\Omega )$$\end{document}L2(Ω) for bounded subsets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}Ω⊂R2. We split the commutator into\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C=C_1-C_2, $$\end{document}C=C1-C2,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _n$$\end{document}Θn be a sequence that is uniformly bounded in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2.
